Fundaments of epimorphisms of profinite groups
Dan Haran

Abstract
We propose and develop a theory that allows to characterize epimorphisms of profinite groups in terms of indecomposable epimorphisms.



Introduction

A central theme in Galois theory is
to understand a profinite group through
a series of its distinguished closed normal subgroups
(
[
E1
]
,

[
E2
]
,

[
CE
]
,

[
EM
]
,

[
MRT
]
);
one could add to this topic
also the efforts to deduce properties of profinite groups
from their distinguished quotients
(
[
MS
]
,
[
MRT
]
).
Although the research in this area is far from its peak,
sometimes even a more general and a more ambitious goal is required -
a characterization of an epimorphism
of profinite groups.
(This is more general in the sense that
a profinite group
G
can be identified with the epimorphism

G\twoheadrightarrow 1
.)
In fact,
the original motivation for this work
has been to describe
a smallest embedding cover of a profinite group
and give a group-theoretic proof of its uniqueness
(
[
FJ
, Problem 36.2.25]
).

In this paper we provide a framework for a characterization
of epimorphisms,
the theory of
fundaments
.

Given an epimorphism

\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
,
its
fundament kernel

M(\pi)

is the intersection of the maximal normal subgroups of
H

properly contained in
\Ker(\pi)
;
quotienting by
M(\pi)
yields the
fundament

\bar{\pi}
,
and
\pi
is called
fundamental
when
M(\pi)=1
.
Iterating this construction produces
the
fundament kernel series

M_{0}(\pi)\geq M_{1}(\pi)\geq M_{2}(\pi)\geq\cdots

with associated
fundament series
of epimorphisms

\cdots\xrightarrow{\pi_{3}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow G_{2}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow G_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow G_{0}=G,

where
G_{i}=H/M_{i}(\pi)
and
each
\pi_{k}
is the fundament of the preceding quotient
\pi_{k-1}
.
The intersection of all
M_{i}(\pi)
is trivial,
so
\pi
is recovered as the inverse limit of its successive fundaments.
Moreover,
two epimorphisms onto
G

are isomorphic
if and only if
their fundament series are isomorphic.

Thus we are left with the task
to classify fundamental epimorphisms,
up to isomorphism,
and to decide
when a series of fundamental epimorphisms
as above
is a fundament series of its inverse limit.
This is, essentially, what this paper is about.

It turns out that
fundamental epimorphisms
are fiber products of
(possibly infinitely many, even uncountably many)
indecomposable epimorphisms.
An epimorphism

\varphi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

is
indecomposable

if it is not an isomorphism
and every factorization

H\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow E\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow G

forces one of
\psi
or
\rho
to be an isomorphism.
Equivalently,

\Ker\varphi
is
a minimal normal subgroup of
H

(and in particular finite).

Obviously,
two fiber products of indecomposable epimorphisms
onto the same group
G

are isomorphic,
if
the cardinality of factors isomorphic to
\eta

is the same in both of them,
for every indecomposable epimorphism
\eta
onto
G
.
The converse is not true:
Let
C_{n}
be the cyclic group of order
n
.
Then

\eta_{0}\colon C_{2}\times C_{2}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}

and

\eta_{1}\colon C_{4}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}

are indecomposable.
The fiber product
of two copies of
\eta_{1}

and
the fiber product
of
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}

are both isomorphic
to the same epimorphism

C_{4}\times C_{2}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}
.

Fortunately,
this problem arises only with
indecomposable epimorphisms onto
G

with the same abelian kernel,
which is then a simple
G
-module
A
.
The kernel of a fiber product of such epimorphisms
is the direct product of copies of
A
.
Sections
4
develops a duality theory between
isotypic profinite semisimple modules of type
A

and
vector spaces over the finite field
\End_{G}(A)
.
Then Section
5

develops a duality theory between
fiber products of
indecomposable epimorphisms onto
G

with kernel
A

and
linear maps from these vector spaces into
H^{2}(G,A)
.
This allows us to classify the former by the latter.
Then,
in Section
8
,
we succeed to characterize fundamental epimorphisms
by invariants introduced there.

Throughout the whole paper
we use extensively
commutative squares

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{0}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(0.1)

of epimorphisms.
The key notions,
introduced in Section
2

are
cartesian squares

—(
0.1
) is cartesian
if
H
is the fiber product of
\theta_{0}
and
\pi^{\prime}
,
or, equivalently,
if
\theta
maps
\Ker\pi
bijectively onto
\Ker\pi^{\prime}
—,
and the
compactness
of cartesian squares,
—cartesian (
0.1
) is compact
if there is no proper subgroup
E
of
H

such that

\pi(E)=G
and
\theta(E)=H^{\prime}
—,
which later, in Section
3
,
extends to general fiber products.
We have also found very useful the notion of
a
semi-cartesian
square
—(
0.1
) is semi-cartesian
if
\theta
maps
\Ker\pi
onto
\Ker\pi^{\prime}
.

The theory of fundaments
characterizes epimorphisms.
But it
actually achieves more.
Given epimorphisms

\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
,

\pi^{\prime}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
,
and

\theta_{0}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
,
we find the exact conditions
on the fundament series of
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
,
such that there exists an epimorphism

\theta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}

with (
0.1
) semi-cartesian.

This generalization is essential
for an application of
the theory developed here.
In the forthcoming work
[
H2
]

we give
a group-theoretic proof
of the uniqueness of the smallest embedding cover.
Further applications will include
characterizations of projective and free profinite groups
via fundaments.

We fix the following notation and conventions
throughout the whole paper:

We use the word ‘family’ for indexed family.

We use
\twoheadrightarrow
to denote an epimorphism of profinite groups.

An epimorphism
\eta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

is sometimes called
a
cover
(of
G
)
and sometimes
an
extension
(of
G
).
Another epimorphism

\pi\colon E\twoheadrightarrow G

is
dominated by
\eta

(or
\eta
dominates
\pi
),
denoted
\pi\preccurlyeq\eta
,
if there is

\psi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow E

such that

\eta=\pi\circ\psi
.
We say that

\pi,\psi
are
isomorphic
,
denoted
\pi\cong_{G}\psi
,
if there is an isomorphism

\psi\colon H\to E

such that

\eta=\pi\circ\psi
.

All groups in this article
are profinite,
and homomorphisms between groups are continuous;
a subgroup of a group is a closed subgroup.

We denote by
\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}

the direct product of a family
of groups

(K_{i})_{i\in I}
.

1
Indecomposable epimorphisms

Definition 1.1
.

An epimorphism
\varphi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
is

indecomposable

if it is not an isomorphism and
whenever
\psi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow E
and
\rho\colon E\twoheadrightarrow G

satisfy

\varphi=\rho\circ\psi
,
then either
\psi
or
\rho
is an isomorphism.

Let
H
be a profinite group.
For
M\triangleleft H
let

\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)
denote the set of
the maximal ones among
all normal subgroups of
H

strictly contained in
M
.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
N\leq M
be closed normal subgroups of a profinite group
H
.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

N\in\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)
;

(b)

M/N
is a minimal normal subgroup of
H/N

(in particular,
M/N\neq 1
);

(c)

the canonical map

H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/M

is indecomposable.

If (a), (b), or (c) holds,
then
N
is open in
M
,
i.e.,
M/N
is a finite group.

Proof.

Indeed,

\displaystyle(a)\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad N<M\quad\quad\&\quad(\not\exists L\triangleleft H)\ N<L<M

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad M/N\neq 1\quad\&\quad(\not\exists{\bar{L}}\triangleleft H/N)\ 1<{\bar{L}}<M/N\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(b).

(a)
\Rightarrow
(c):
If
H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/M
is a composition of two epimorphisms,
then, without loss of generality,
they are the canonical maps

H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/L
and
H/L\twoheadrightarrow H/M
,
where
L\triangleleft H
and
N\leq L\leq M
.
By (a), either
N=L
, in which case
H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/L
is an isomorphism,
or
L=M
, in which case
H/L\twoheadrightarrow H/M
is an isomorphism.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(a):
Let
L\triangleleft H
with
N\leq L\leq M
.
Then
H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/M
is the composition of

H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/L
and
H/L\twoheadrightarrow H/M
.
If the former is an isomorphism, then
N=L
;
if the latter is an isomorphism, then
L=M
.
This gives (a).

Assume (b) and put
{\bar{H}}=H/N
and
{\bar{M}}=M/N
.
As
{\bar{M}}\neq 1
,
there is an open
{\bar{U}}\triangleleft{\bar{H}}
such that

{\bar{U}}\cap{\bar{M}}<{\bar{M}}
.
As
{\bar{U}}\cap{\bar{M}}\triangleleft{\bar{H}}
,
by (b) we have
{\bar{U}}\cap{\bar{M}}=1
.
Thus
1
is open in
{\bar{M}}
,
that is,
{\bar{M}}
is finite.
∎

Remark 1.3
.

Let

\varphi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be an epimorphism.
It follows from Lemma
1.2

(with
N=1
) that:

(a)

\varphi
is indecomposable
if and only if

\Ker\varphi
is a minimal normal subgroup of
H
;

(b)
If
\varphi
is indecomposable,
then
\Ker\varphi
is finite.

(c)
A minimal normal subgroup of
H
is finite.

Furthermore

(d)
If
1\neq M\triangleleft H
, then

\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)\neq\emptyset
.

Indeed,
as
M\neq 1
,
by
[
FJ
, Remark 1.2.1(c)]

there is an open
U\triangleleft H
such that

N_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=M\cap U<M
.
Thus,

N_{0}\triangleleft H
is a proper subgroup of
M
.
Since
N_{0}
is open in
M
,
there are only finitely many
N\triangleleft H

with
N_{0}\leq N<M
.
A maximal one among them is in
\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)
.

(e)
However,
by (c),
non-trivial torsion free profinite groups
do not have minimal normal subgroups.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{H}}
be an epimorphism.
Let
M\triangleleft H
and
{\bar{M}}=\pi(M)
.

(a)

If
M
is a minimal normal subgroup of
H

and
{\bar{M}}\neq 1
,
then
{\bar{M}}
is a minimal normal subgroup of
{\bar{H}}

and

\pi|_{M}\colon M\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{M}}
is an isomorphism.

(b)

Suppose that
M\cap\Ker\pi=1
.
If
{\bar{M}}
is a minimal normal subgroup of
{\bar{H}}
,
then
M
is a minimal normal subgroup of
H
.

(c)

Let
{\bar{N}}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bar{M}}({\bar{H}})
.
Then
N\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\pi^{-1}({\bar{N}})\cap M

is in
\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)
and
\pi(N)={\bar{N}}
.

Proof.

Let
\rho\colon M\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{M}}
be the restriction of
\pi
.

(a)
Let
1\leq{\bar{L}}<{\bar{M}}
be a normal subgroup of
{\bar{H}}
.
Then
\pi^{-1}({\bar{L}})\triangleleft H
,
hence
L\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\pi^{-1}({\bar{L}})\cap M\triangleleft H
.
But
L=\rho^{-1}({\bar{L}})
.
Hence
L<M
.
As
M
is minimal and
1\leq L<M
, we have
L=1
,
hence
\rho
is injective and

{\bar{L}}=\rho(L)=1
.
Thus
{\bar{M}}
is minimal.

(b)
Let
1<L\leq M
be a normal subgroup of
H
.
Then
{\bar{L}}=\pi(L)
is normal in
{\bar{H}}
and
{\bar{L}}\leq{\bar{M}}
.
By assumption,
\rho\colon M\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{M}}
is an isomorphism,
hence
{\bar{L}}=\rho(L)
is not trivial.
As
{\bar{M}}
is minimal, we have
{\bar{L}}={\bar{M}}
.
Apply
\rho^{-1}
to get
L=M
.

(c)
Let
N_{0}=\pi^{-1}({\bar{N}})
.
Then
N_{0}\triangleleft H
,
hence
N=N_{0}\cap M\triangleleft H
.
But
N=\rho^{-1}({\bar{N}})
,
hence
\pi(N)=\rho(N)={\bar{N}}<{\bar{M}}
,
whence
N<M
,
and
N
is open in
M
,
because
{\bar{N}}
is open in
{\bar{M}}
.
Thus,

N\triangleleft H
is a proper subgroup of
M
.
If there were
L\triangleleft H
such that
N<L<M
,
then
{\bar{L}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\rho(L)=\pi(L)\triangleleft{\bar{H}}

and
{\bar{N}}<{\bar{L}}<{\bar{M}}
,
because
\Ker\rho\leq N
,
a contradiction.
Thus
N\in\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)
.
∎

Remark 1.5
.

Let
M\neq 1
be a minimal normal subgroup of a profinite group
H
.
By Remark
1.3
(c)
there is an open
U\triangleleft H
such that
U\cap M=1
.
Then
{\bar{H}}=H/U
is a finite group.
By Lemma
1.4
(a),
the image
{\bar{M}}
of
M
in
{\bar{H}}

is a minimal normal subgroup of
{\bar{H}}
.
Therefore (
[
DM
, Theorem 4.3A]
)

{\bar{M}}
is the direct product of finite simple groups
conjugate to each other in
{\bar{H}}
.
As the quotient map
H\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{H}}
restricts to an isomorphism

M\to{\bar{M}}
,
M
is the direct product of finite simple groups
conjugate to each other in
H
.

Let
G
be a profinite group.
A finite
G
-module
A\neq 0

is
simple

if
A
has no submodule
B

with
0\subsetneqq B\subsetneqq A
.

Let

\varphi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be an epimorphism of profinite groups
and let
K
be its kernel.
If
K
is abelian,
it is customary to view
K
as a
G
-module,
with
G
-action induced from the conjugation in
H
;
if
L\leq K
,
then
L
is a
G
-submodule of
K

if and only if

L
is a normal subgroup of
H
contained in
K
.
One can generalize this to a non-abelian
K

(but abelian
L
):

Lemma 1.6
.

Let

\varphi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be an epimorphism of profinite groups
and let

L
be a normal subgroup of
H
contained in
Z(\Ker\varphi)
.
Then

(a)

The restriction of the conjugation in
H
to
L

defines a homomorphism

\hat{\rho}\colon H\to\Aut(L)

such that

\hat{\rho}(\Ker\varphi)=1
.
Hence,

\hat{\rho}
induces a homomorphism

\rho\colon G\to\Aut(L)
,
which turns
L
into a
G
-module.
The action is given by

{}^{g}x=hxh^{-1}
,
where
g\in G
,
x\in L
, and

h
is an arbitrary element of
H
such that
\varphi(h)=g
.

(b)

A\leq Z(\Ker\varphi)

is a minimal normal subgroup of
H

if and only if

A
is a simple
G
-module.
If this is the case,
then
A
is finite.

(c)

If
A=\Ker\varphi
is abelian,
then

\varphi
is indecomposable
if and only if

A
is a simple
G
-module.

(d)

Let

\varphi^{\prime}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G

and

\varepsilon\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}

be epimorphisms
such that

\varphi^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon=\varphi
.
Then

G
acts on
\varepsilon(L)
as a subgroup of
Z(\Ker\varphi^{\prime})

and

\varepsilon|_{L}\colon L\twoheadrightarrow\varepsilon(L)

is
G
-equivariant.

(e)

Suppose there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

such that

\varepsilon(\Ker\varphi)=\Ker\varphi^{\prime}
.
Then

G^{\prime}
acts on
\varepsilon(L)
as a subgroup of
Z(\Ker\varphi^{\prime})

via
\varphi^{\prime}
, as in (a),
so that

{}^{\pi(g)}(\varepsilon(x))=\varepsilon({}^{g}x)
,
for all
x\in L
and
g\in G
.

Proof.

Let
K=\Ker\varphi
.

(a)
As
L\leq Z(K)
, we have
[L,K]=1
, and hence

\hat{\rho}(K)=1
.

(b)
Obviously,

A\leq Z(K)

is a
G
-module
if and only if

A
is a normal subgroup of
H
.
Applying the attribute ”minimal”
to both notions
we get the equivalence.
The finiteness follows from
Remark
1.3
(c).

(c)
This follows from (b) by
Remark
1.3
(a).

(e)
Let
K^{\prime}=\Ker\varphi^{\prime}
.
We have

\varepsilon(K)=K^{\prime}
,
hence

\varepsilon(L)
is a normal subgroup of
H^{\prime}

contained in
Z(K^{\prime})
.
So
G^{\prime}
acts on it, by (a).
Let

g\in G
and
x\in L
, and choose

h\in H
such that
\varphi(h)=g
.
Then
\varphi^{\prime}(\varepsilon(h))=\pi(g)
,
hence

{}^{\pi(g)}(\varepsilon(x))=\varepsilon(h)\varepsilon(x)(\varepsilon(h))^{-1}=\varepsilon(hxh^{-1})=\varepsilon({}^{g}x)
.

(d)
This follows from (e) with
G^{\prime}=G
and
\pi=\mathrm{id}_{G}
.
Notice that

\varepsilon(K)=\Ker\varphi^{\prime}
.
∎

2
Cartesian squares

Definition 2.1
.

Consider a commutative diagram

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

(2.1)

Let
p=\alpha\circ\beta=\varphi\circ\eta

and put

K_{1}=\Ker\beta
,

K_{2}=\Ker\eta
,
and

K=\Ker p
.
We call (
2.1
)
a
cartesian square

(
[
FJ
, Definition 25.2.2]
)
if it satisfies the following equivalent conditions:

(a)

Up to an isomorphism,

H
is the fiber product

B\times_{A}G
of
\alpha
and
\varphi
,
with coordinate projections
\beta
and
\eta
.

(b)

Given homomorphisms

\beta^{\prime}\colon E\to B

and

\eta^{\prime}\colon E\to G

such that

\alpha\circ\beta^{\prime}=\varphi\circ\eta^{\prime}
,
there is a unique homomorphism

\varepsilon\colon E\to H

such that

\beta^{\prime}=\beta\circ\varepsilon

and

\eta^{\prime}=\eta\circ\varepsilon
.

(c)

K=K_{1}\times K_{2}
,
that is,

K_{1}\cap K_{2}=1

and

K=K_{1}K_{2}
.

(d)

\beta
maps
\Ker\eta
isomorphically onto
\Ker\alpha
.

(e)

For all
b\in B
,
g\in G

such that

\alpha(b)=\varphi(g)

there is a unique
h\in H

such that

\beta(h)=b
and
\eta(h)=g
.

The equivalence of the conditions is

[
FJ
, Proposition 25.2.1,
Lemma 25.2.4,
and
Lemma 25.2.5]
,
except for
(e)
\Rightarrow
(c),
which is trivial,
and

(d)
\Rightarrow
(c):
As
\beta|_{K_{2}}
is injective,

K_{1}\cap K_{2}=1
.
As
\beta(K_{2})=\Ker\alpha
,
we have

K=\Ker(\alpha\circ\beta)=\beta^{-1}(\Ker\alpha)=(\Ker\beta)K_{2}=K_{1}K_{2}
.

Corollary 2.2
.

In a cartesian square (
2.1
)

\alpha
is indecomposable
if and only if

\eta
is indecomposable.

Proof.

This follows from
(a) and (b) of
Lemma
1.4

by Remark
1.3
(a).
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let (
2.1
) be a cartesian square.
Then
\beta
maps
the lattice of normal subgroups of
H
contained in
\Ker\eta

isomorphically onto
the lattice of normal subgroups of
B
contained in
\Ker\alpha
.

Proof.

The restriction
\delta\colon\Ker\eta\to\Ker\alpha

of
\beta

is an isomorphism, so it maps
the lattice of subgroups of
\Ker\eta

isomorphically onto
the lattice of subgroups of
\Ker\alpha
.
If
L\leq\Ker\eta
is normal in
H
,
then
\delta(L)=\beta(L)
is normal in
B
.
Conversely, if
L\leq\Ker\alpha
is normal in
B
,
then
\delta^{-1}(L)=\beta^{-1}(L)\cap\Ker\eta
is normal in
H
.
∎

We will need also a weakening of the cartesian property:

Definition 2.4
.

A commutative diagram
(
2.1
)
is a
semi-cartesian square

if it satisfies the following equivalent properties:

(a)

\Ker(\varphi\circ\eta)=(\Ker\beta)(\Ker(\varphi)
.

(b)

\beta(\Ker\eta)=\Ker\alpha
.

(c)

\eta(\Ker\beta)=\Ker\varphi
.

(d)

(Push-out)
Given homomorphisms

\alpha^{\prime}\colon B\to A^{\prime}

and

\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\to A^{\prime}

such that

\alpha^{\prime}\circ\beta=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\eta
,
there is a unique homomorphism

\lambda\colon A\to A^{\prime}

such that

\alpha^{\prime}=\lambda\circ\alpha

and

\varphi^{\prime}=\lambda\circ\varphi
.

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}}

(2.2)

(e)

If
epimorphisms

\alpha^{\prime}
,

\varphi^{\prime}
,
and

\pi

make the following diagram commute

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

(2.3)

then
\pi
is an isomorphism.

(f)

For all
b\in B
,
g\in G

such that

\alpha(b)=\varphi(g)

there is
h\in H

such that

\beta(h)=b
and
\eta(h)=g
.

Proof of the equivalence of the properties.

Put
p=\varphi\circ\eta=\alpha\circ\beta
.
We may assume that

B=H/K_{1}
,

G=H/K_{2}
,

A=H/K
,
where
K_{1},K_{2}\leq K
;
\beta
,
\eta
, and
p\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H/K

are the quotient maps;
and

\alpha,\varphi

are the maps induced from

\eta,\beta
,
respectively.

(a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b):

\beta(\Ker\eta)=\beta(K_{2})=K_{1}K_{2}/K_{1}

and

\Ker\alpha=K/K_{1}
.
This gives the equivalence.

(a)
\Leftrightarrow
(c):

\eta(\Ker\beta)=\eta(K_{1})=K_{1}K_{2}/K_{2}

and

\Ker\varphi=K/K_{1}
.
This gives the equivalence.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(d):
The uniqueness of
\lambda
follows already from

\lambda\circ\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}
.
Let

p^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}\circ\beta=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\eta
.
We have
K_{1},K_{2}\leq\Ker p^{\prime}
,
hence
\Ker p=K_{1}K_{2}\leq\Ker p^{\prime}
.
So
there is
\lambda\colon A\to A^{\prime}

such that

\lambda\circ p=p^{\prime}
.
Thus

\lambda\circ\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha^{\prime}\circ\beta
,
whence

\lambda\circ\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
.
Similarly

\lambda\circ\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}
.

(d)
\Rightarrow
(a):
We have shown in ‘(a)
\Rightarrow
(d)’
that if we replace

\alpha
,
\varphi

by

H/K_{1}\to H/K_{1}K_{2}
,
H/K_{2}\to H/K_{1}K_{2}
,
then the square has the universal property (d).
This universal property implies
the uniqueness of

\alpha
,
\varphi
in (
2.1
),
up to an isomorphism.
Hence
K_{1}K_{2}=K
.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(e):
We may assume that

A^{\prime}=H/L
,
where
K_{1},K_{2}\leq L\leq K
,
and

\alpha^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\pi

are the quotient maps

H/K_{1}\twoheadrightarrow H/L
,

H/K_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H/L
,
and

H/L\twoheadrightarrow H/K
,
respectively.
Then
K_{1}K_{2}\leq L\leq K
.
By (a),

L=K
,
hence
\pi
is an isomorphism.

(e)
\Rightarrow
(a):
Let
A^{\prime}=H/K_{1}K_{2}

and let

\alpha^{\prime}\colon H/K_{1}\twoheadrightarrow H/K_{1}K_{2}
,

\varphi^{\prime}\colon H/K_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H/K_{1}K_{2}
,

\pi\colon H/K_{1}K_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H/K

be the quotient maps.
As
\pi
is an isomorphism,

K_{1}K_{2}=K
.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(f):
Choose
h^{\prime}\in H
such that
\beta(h^{\prime})=b

and let
g^{\prime}=\eta(h^{\prime})
.
Then
\varphi(g^{\prime})=\varphi(\eta(h^{\prime}))=\alpha(b)=\varphi(g)
,
hence there is
g^{\prime\prime}\in\Ker\varphi

such that
g=g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}
.
By (c) there is
h^{\prime\prime}\in\Ker\beta
such that

\eta(h^{\prime\prime})=g^{\prime\prime}
.
Then
\eta(h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime})=g

and
\beta(h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime})=b
.

(f)
\Rightarrow
(c):
Clearly
\eta(\Ker\beta)\leq\Ker\varphi
.
Conversely,
let
g\in\Ker\varphi
.
Then
\beta(1)=\varphi(g)
.
By (f) there is
h\in H

such that

h\in\Ker\beta
and
\eta(h)=g
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Clearly,
if
\alpha
is an isomorphism,
then (
2.1
)
is semi-cartesian.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let (
2.1
) be a commutative diagram
with
\alpha
indecomposable.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

(
2.1
)
is a semi-cartesian square.

(b)

There is no
\gamma\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

such that

\gamma\circ\eta=\beta
.

(c)

\Ker\eta\not\subseteq\Ker\beta
.

Proof.

We prove the equivalence of the negations of these assertions:

(
\neg
a)
\Rightarrow
(
\neg
b):
There is a commutative diagram (
2.3
)
in which
\pi
is not an isomorphism.
As
\alpha
is indecomposable,

\alpha^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
Then
\gamma:=(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

satisfies

\gamma\circ\eta=\beta
.

(
\neg
b)
\Rightarrow
(
\neg
a):
If there is

\gamma\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

such that

\gamma\circ\eta=\beta
,
put

A^{\prime}=B
,

\alpha^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{B}
,

\varphi^{\prime}=\gamma
,
and

\pi=\alpha
.
Then (
2.3
) commutes,
but
\pi
is not an isomorphism.
So
(
2.1
)
is not semi-cartesian,
by Definition
2.4
(e).

(
\neg
b)
\Leftrightarrow
(
\neg
a):
By the first isomorphism theorem,
there is

\gamma\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

such that

\gamma\circ\eta=\beta
,
if and only if

\Ker\eta\subseteq\Ker\beta
.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose that
\varphi

in a semi-cartesian square
(
2.1
)
is the composition

\varphi=\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}

of two epimorphisms.
Then
(
\varphi_{1}\circ\eta,\varphi_{2},\beta,\alpha)

is a semi-cartesian square.

Proof.

By assumption,

(\Ker\beta)(\Ker\eta)=\Ker(\alpha\circ\beta)
.
But

\Ker\eta\leq\Ker(\varphi_{1}\circ\eta)\leq\Ker(\varphi\circ\eta)=\Ker(\alpha\circ\beta),

hence

(\Ker\beta)(\Ker(\varphi_{1}\circ\eta))=\Ker(\alpha\circ\beta)
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Consider two commutative diagrams

\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

(2.4)

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{A}

(2.5)

and let

\psi\colon H^{\prime}\to H

be a homomorphism
such that

\beta^{\prime}=\beta\circ\psi

and

\eta^{\prime}=\eta\circ\psi
.

(a)

If
\psi
is surjective, then
(
2.4
) is semi-cartesian
if and only if
(
2.5
) is semi-cartesian.

(b)

If
(
2.4
) is semi-cartesian
and
(
2.5
) is cartesian,
then
\psi
is surjective.

Proof.

(a)
The universal property (d) of Definition
2.4

holds for
(
2.4
)
if and only if
it holds for
(
2.5
).

(b)
Let
h\in H
.
Then

\alpha(\beta(h))=\varphi(\eta(h))
.
By Definition
2.4
(f)
there is

h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}

such that

\beta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})=\beta(h)

and

\eta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})=\eta(h)
,
that is,

\beta(\psi(h^{\prime}))=\beta(h)

and

\eta(\psi(h^{\prime}))=\eta(h)
.
By Definition
2.1
(e),

\psi(h^{\prime})=h
.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

A cartesian square
of epimorphisms of profinite groups
(
2.1
)
is
compact
if
it satisfies the following equivalent properties:

(a)

No proper closed subgroup of
H
is mapped
by
\beta
onto
B

and by
\eta
onto
G
.

(b)

If
E
is a profinite group
and
\beta^{\prime}\colon E\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\eta^{\prime}\colon E\twoheadrightarrow G

satisfy

\alpha\circ\beta^{\prime}=\varphi\circ\eta^{\prime}
,
then the unique homomorphism

\psi\colon E\to H
such that

\beta\circ\psi=\beta^{\prime}
and
\eta\circ\psi=\eta^{\prime}

is surjective.

(c)

If

\alpha^{\prime}\colon B\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
,

\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
,
and

\pi\colon A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A

satisfy

\pi\circ\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha

and

\pi\circ\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
,
then
\pi
is an isomorphism.

(d)

There are no

\alpha^{\prime}\colon B\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
,

\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
,
and an indecomposable

\pi\colon A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A

such that

\pi\circ\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha

and

\pi\circ\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
.

Proof of the equivalence of the properties.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):
Let
H_{0}=\psi(E)
.
Then
\beta(H_{0})=\beta^{\prime}(E)=B

and
\eta(H_{0})=\eta^{\prime}(E)=G
,
hence, by (a),
H_{0}=H
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a):
Let
E\leq H
such that

\beta(E)=B

and
\eta(E)=G
.
Let
\beta^{\prime}=\beta|_{B}
,

\eta^{\prime}=\eta|_{E}
,
and
let
\psi\colon E\to H
be the inclusion.
Then

\beta\circ\psi=\beta^{\prime}
and
\eta\circ\psi=\eta^{\prime}
,
hence, by (b),
\psi
is surjective.
Thus
E=H
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(c):
Lemma
2.8
(a).
(c)
\Rightarrow
(b):
Lemma
2.8
(b).

(c)
\Rightarrow
(d):
Clear, because indecomposable epimorphism is not an isomorphism.

(d)
\Rightarrow
(c):
Let
\alpha^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\pi

such that

\pi\circ\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha

and

\pi\circ\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
.
If
\pi
is not an isomorphism,
then it is the composition of two epimorphisms

\pi=\pi^{\prime\prime}\circ\pi^{\prime}
,
with
\pi^{\prime\prime}
indecomposable,
hence

\pi^{\prime\prime}\circ(\pi^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha

and

\pi^{\prime\prime}\circ(\pi^{\prime}\circ\varphi^{\prime})=\varphi
,
a contradiction to (d).
So,
\pi
is an isomorphism.
∎

(2.6)

(a)

If two of the squares are cartesian,
then so is the third one.

(b)

If all the three squares are cartesian,

).

Lemma 2.11
.

(a)

If (
2.1
) is semi-cartesian,
then the right-handed square in (
2.6
)
is semi-cartesian.

(b)

If both the right-handed

).

.

and

,

.
∎

Here is a fundamental example of a compact cartesian square:

Proposition 2.12
.

A cartesian square (
2.1
)
with
\alpha
indecomposable
is compact
if and only if
there is no
\gamma\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

such that

\alpha\circ\gamma=\varphi
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.9
(c),
square (
2.1
)
is not compact
if and only if

(c)

there are

\alpha^{\prime}\colon B\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
,

\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
,
and

\pi\colon A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A

such that

\pi\circ\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
,

\pi\circ\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
,
and

\pi
is not an isomorphism.

If (c) holds,
then, as
\alpha
is indecomposable and
\pi
is not isomorphism,

\alpha^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
Then
\gamma:=(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\varphi^{\prime}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

satisfies

\alpha\circ\gamma=\varphi
.

Conversely,
if

\gamma\colon G\twoheadrightarrow B

satisfies

\alpha\circ\gamma=\varphi
,
then

A^{\prime}=B
,

\alpha^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{B}
,

\varphi^{\prime}=\gamma
,
and

\pi=\alpha

satisfy (c).
∎

Corollary 2.13
.

If (
2.1
) is a non-compact cartesian square,
then there is a diagram (
2.6
)
of cartesian squares,
in which
\alpha^{\prime}
is indecomposable
and the right-handed square is not compact.

Proof.

By Definition
2.9

there are

\gamma\colon B\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}
,

,
and an indecomposable

\alpha^{\prime}\colon B^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A

such that

and

\alpha^{\prime}\circ\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
.
By Remark
2.10
(c),
there exists a commutative diagram (
2.6
)
with cartesian squares.
By Proposition
2.12

the right-handed square is not compact.
∎

.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let

(H_{i},\rho_{ji}\colon H_{j}\twoheadrightarrow H_{i})_{i,j\in I}

and

(G_{i},\pi_{ji}\colon G_{j}\twoheadrightarrow G_{i})_{i,j\in I}

over the same directed set

.

\beta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}

(

) below

\textstyle{H_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{j}}

\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{i}}

(2.8)

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{i}}
\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{i}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{G_{i}}

(2.9)

is a cartesian square
for all

.

,
with projections

\rho_{i}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H_{i}

and

\pi_{i}\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}
,
respectively.
Then
(\beta_{i})_{i\in I}
induce

\beta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

such that
(
2.9
) above
is a cartesian square,
for all
i\in I
.
If
(
2.8
)
are compact,
for all
j\geq i
,
then
(
2.9
)
are compact,
for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Fix
i\in I
.

Let
h_{i}\in H_{i}
and
g\in G

such that

\pi_{i}(g)=\beta_{i}(h_{i})
.
For every
j\geq i

put
g_{j}=\pi_{j}(g)
.
There is a unique
h_{j}\in H_{j}

such that

\beta_{j}(h_{j})=g_{j}
and
\rho_{ji}(h_{j})=h_{i}
.
If
k\geq j\geq i
,
then

\beta_{j}(\rho_{kj}(h_{k}))=\pi_{kj}(\beta_{k}(h_{k}))=\pi_{kj}(g_{k})=g_{j}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho_{ki}(\rho_{kj}(h_{k}))=\rho_{ki}(h_{k})=h_{i},

so, by the uniqueness of
h_{j}
,

\rho_{kj}(h_{k})=h_{j}
.
So there is a unique
h\in H

such that

\rho_{j}(h)=h_{j}
for every
j\geq i
.
Obviously, this
h
is the unique element of
H

such that

\beta(h)=g
and
\rho_{i}(h)=h_{i}
.
Therefore
(
2.9
)
is cartesian.

Let
H^{\prime}\leq H

such that

\beta(H^{\prime})=G

and

\rho_{i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}
.
Put
H^{\prime}_{j}=\rho_{j}(H^{\prime})

for all
j\in I
.
Then

\beta_{j}(H^{\prime}j)=G_{j}

and

\rho_{ji}(H^{\prime}_{j})=H^{\prime}_{i}

for all
j\geq i
.
As (
2.8
) is compact,

H^{\prime}_{j}=H_{j}

for all
j\in I
.
Hence

H^{\prime}=H
.
Therefore (
2.9
) is compact.
∎

3
Multiple fiber products

Let us fix a family

\mathcal{H}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\to G)_{i\in I}

of homomorphisms,
for the moment

nonempty
(until Remark
3.1
(b)).
Consider the category

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})=\{(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})|\,H\textnormal{ is a group, }{\bar{p}}\colon H\to G\textnormal{ is a homomorphism, and }\\
p_{i}\colon H\to H_{i}\textnormal{ is a homomorphism}\textnormal{ such that }\eta_{i}\circ p_{i}={\bar{p}},\textnormal{ for every }i\in I\}.

A morphism

p\colon(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\to(H^{\prime},\{p^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}}^{\prime})

in this category
is a homomorphism

p\colon H\to H^{\prime}

such that

p_{i}=p^{\prime}_{i}\circ p
for every
i\in I
;
notice that this implies that

{\bar{p}}={\bar{p}}^{\prime}\circ p
.
Indeed,

{\bar{p}}^{\prime}\circ p=\eta_{i}\circ p^{\prime}_{i}\circ p=\eta_{i}\circ p_{i}={\bar{p}}
.

The
fiber product of
\mathcal{H}

is the terminal object

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\}_{i\in I},\eta_{I})

in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
.
In other words,

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\}_{i\in I},\eta_{I})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
,
and if

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
,
then there is a unique homomorphism

p_{I}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\circ p_{I}=p_{i}
for every
i\in I
.
We write

H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
if this
p_{I}
is an isomorphism.

The fiber product exists and is unique,
up to a unique isomorphism.
Namely,
(
[
BHH
, Section 2]
)
let

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\Big\{(h_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}H_{i}|\,\eta_{i}(h_{i})=\eta_{j}(h_{j})\textnormal{ for all }i,j\in I\Big\},

(3.1)

let

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to H_{j}

be the projection on the
j
-th coordinate,
for every
j\in I
,
and let

{\eta_{I}=\eta_{j}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to G}
,
for any
j\in I

(the definition is independent of the choice of
j
).
Then

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\}_{i\in I},\eta_{I})

has the above universal property.
(We will usually use this concrete presentation.)
Its uniqueness is an abstract nonsense.

We sometimes write

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I})
)
instead of

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})
.
In particular, in the case of a fiber product,
we usually abbreviate

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\}_{i\in I},\eta_{I})

or

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\}_{i\in I})

to

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.

We call

\eta_{I}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to G

the
structure map of
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.

In Remark
3.2
below
we introduce the notation

K_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{j\}}
,
for every
j\in I
.

Remark 3.1
.

(a)
If
I=\{j\}
is a singleton,
then

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=H_{j}

and
\eta_{I}=\eta_{j}
.

(b)
The definition of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

does not change,
if we replace
I
with
I^{\prime}\supset I
,
where
\eta_{i}=\mathrm{id}_{G}
for all
i\in I^{\prime}\smallsetminus I
.
Indeed,
the fiber product of the family

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\to G)_{i\in I^{\prime}}

is also the fiber product of
\mathcal{H}
.
This presentation allows us to define

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
and
\eta_{I}

also for
I=\emptyset

–
simply replace
I
with
\{j\}
,
where
\eta_{j}=\mathrm{id}_{G}\colon G\to G
.
Thus, by (a),

\underset{i\in\emptyset\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=G
and
\eta_{\emptyset}=\mathrm{id}_{G}
.
Applying this, we may

drop the assumption
I\neq\emptyset

from now on.

Remark 3.2
.

We list a few easy properties of

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\}_{i\in I},\eta_{I})
:

(a)
Given
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
,
we may form the fiber product

(\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i},\{\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\}_{i\in I},\eta_{I^{\prime}})

of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\to G)_{i\in I^{\prime}}
.
Let
\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

be the projection on the coordinates in
I^{\prime}
.
It is a homomorphism.
We have

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},j}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}
,
for every
j\in I^{\prime}
.

It is easy to see that

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\varprojlim_{I^{\prime}}\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
where
I^{\prime}
runs through the finite subsets of
I
.

(b)
Let
j\in I
.
We identify
\Ker\eta_{j}

via
\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}

with

K_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{(h_{i})_{i\in I}|\,h_{i}\in H_{i}\textnormal{ and }h_{i}=1\textnormal{ for every }i\neq j\}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{j\}}\triangleleft\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}.

Then

\Ker\eta_{I}=\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}
,

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\prod_{i\neq j}K_{i}

for every
j\in I
,
and

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}=\prod_{i\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}K_{i}

for every
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.

(c)
Let
j\in I
such that
\eta_{j}
is surjective
and
\Ker\eta_{j}
is abelian.
Then
K_{j}\leq Z(\prod_{i\in I}K_{i})=Z(\Ker\eta_{I})
.
Hence,
by Lemma
1.6
(a),

K_{j}
and
\Ker\eta_{j}
are
G
-modules.
By Lemma
1.6
(d),
the identification in (b) preserves the
G
-action.
Thus,

\Ker\eta_{j}\cong_{G}K_{j}
.

(d)
The fiber product has the following transitivity property:
If
I
is a disjoint union

I=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I_{\lambda}
,
then

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}\big(\underset{i\in I_{\lambda}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\big).

(Here
the left fiber product on the right handed side
– the one over
\Lambda
–
is associated with the family

(\eta_{I_{\lambda}}\colon\underset{i\in I_{\lambda}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to G)_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
.)

Indeed,
the right handed side satisfies the universal property of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.

(e)
In particular,
if
I=I^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{j\}
, then

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=(\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i})\times_{G}H_{j}
,
that is,

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{j}}
\textstyle{G}

(3.2)

is a cartesian square.

(f)
Let
j\in I
.
If
\eta_{j}
is surjective,
then so is
\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{j\}}
.
If all
\eta_{i}
are surjective,
then so are
\eta_{I}
,

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}
, for all
j\in I
,
and
\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}
, for all
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.

(g)
Assume that all
\eta_{i}
are surjective
and let
j\in I
.
By Corollary
2.2

applied to (
3.2
),

\eta_{j}
is indecomposable
if and only if

\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{j\}}
is indecomposable.

(h)
Let
\pi\colon E\to G
be a homomorphism.
For every
i\in I
let

p_{i}\colon H_{i}\times_{G}E\to E

be the projection on the second
coordinate.
Then

(p_{i})_{i\in I}
gives rise to the fiber product

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{E}}}{{\prod}_{E}}(H_{i}\times_{G}E)
.
It is easy to see that

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{E}}}{{\prod}_{E}}(H_{i}\times_{G}E)=(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i})\times_{G}E
.

We will be interested in the question,
when

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})

is a fiber product of
\mathcal{H}
.
This can be first simplified as follows:

Lemma 3.3
.

Let

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})

and let

p_{I}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

be the induced map.
Then

(a)

\Ker p_{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}\Ker p_{i}
,
hence

p_{I}
is injective
if and only if

\bigcap_{i\in I}\Ker p_{i}=1
.

(b)

p_{I}
is surjective
if and only if
the map
p_{I^{\prime}}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

induced from

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{C}(\{\eta_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}})

is surjective,
for every finite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.

Proof.

(a) Clear from (
3.1
).

(b)
By Remark
3.2
(a),

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\varprojlim_{I^{\prime}}\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
where
I^{\prime}
varies over all finite subsets of
I
;
we have

p_{I^{\prime}}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\circ p_{I}
for every such
I^{\prime}
.
Thus
p_{I}
is the inverse limit of the
(p_{I^{\prime}})
,
from which the assertion follows.
∎

While (a) above
is a satisfactory criterion for injectivity,
(b) only reduces the question of surjectivity to a finite
set of indices.

More can be said about surjectivity when

\mathcal{H}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

is a family of epimorphisms.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
.
Assume that
\eta_{i},p_{i}
are epimorphisms,
for every
i\in I
.
For every
J\subseteq I

let

p_{J}\colon H\to\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

be the unique map induced by

(p_{i})_{i\in J}
.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

p_{I}
is surjective.

(b)

For all

I^{\prime}\subseteq I

and
j\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}
,
if
p_{I^{\prime}}
is surjective then

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{I^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{p_{j}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{j}}
\textstyle{G}

(3.3)

is semi-cartesian.

(c)

The same as (b), but only with
I^{\prime}
finite.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):
Let

I^{\prime}\subseteq I

and

j\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}
,
and put

J=I^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{j\}
.
As
p_{I}
is surjective,
so are

p_{J}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,J}\circ p_{I}

and

p_{I^{\prime}}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\circ p_{I}
.
By Remark
3.2
(e)
the inner square in the following diagram
is cartesian.

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{j}}
\scriptstyle{p_{J}}
\scriptstyle{p_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{J,I}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{j}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{j}}
\textstyle{G}

(3.4)

By Lemma
2.8
(a),
the outer square is semi-cartesian.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(c):
Clear.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(a):
By Lemma
3.3
(b)
it suffices to show that

p_{J}
is surjective for every finite subset
J
of
I
.
We proceed by induction on
|J|
.
If
J=\emptyset
or
|J|=1
,
then
p_{J}
is surjective.

So assume
|J|\geq 2
.
Let
j\in J

and put
I^{\prime}=J\smallsetminus\{j\}
.
By the induction hypothesis,

p_{I^{\prime}}
is surjective.
By Remark
3.2
(e)
the inner square in (
3.4
)
is cartesian.
By Lemma
2.8
(b),

p_{J}
is surjective.
∎

We have the following generalization of

[
FJ
, Lemma 25.2.4]
:

Lemma 3.5
.

Let

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})

with
|I|\geq 2
.
Assume that
\eta_{i},p_{i}
are epimorphisms,
for every
i\in I
.
Put

L_{j}=\bigcap_{i\neq j}\Ker p_{i}
,
for every
j\in I
,
and

L=\Ker{\bar{p}}
.
Then the induced map

p_{I}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is a group isomorphism
if and only if

L=\prod_{i\in I}L_{i}
.

Proof.

If
p_{I}
is an isomorphism,
we may assume that
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and
p_{i}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}
for every
i\in I
.
Then

L_{i}=K_{i}
for every
i\in I

and

L=\Ker\eta_{I}=\prod_{i\in I}L_{i}

by Remark
3.2
(b).

Conversely, assume

L=\prod_{i\in I}L_{i}
.

For
J\subseteq I
let

p_{J}\colon H\to\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

be the unique map induced by

(p_{i})_{i\in J}
.

Claim:

\Ker p_{J}=\prod_{i\in J^{c}}L_{i}
,
where

J^{c}=I\smallsetminus J
.

Indeed,
if
i\neq j
,
then
L_{i}\leq\Ker p_{j}
.
Thus

\prod_{i\in J^{c}}L_{i}\leq\bigcap_{j\in J}\Ker p_{j}=\Ker p_{J}
.
Conversely,
let
h\in\Ker p_{J}
.
Then
h\in\Ker{\bar{p}}=\prod_{i\in I}L_{i}
,
hence

h=h_{J}h^{\prime}
,
where

h_{J}\in\prod_{i\in J}L_{i}

and

h^{\prime}\in\prod_{i\in J^{c}}L_{i}
.
As noted above,

h^{\prime}\in\Ker p_{J}
,
hence

h_{J}=h(h^{\prime})^{-1}\in\Ker p_{J}
.
Thus

h_{J}\in(\prod_{i\in J}L_{i})\cap\Ker p_{J}
.
But

\prod_{i\in J}L_{i}\leq\bigcap_{i\in J^{c}}\Ker p_{i}

and

\Ker p_{J}=\bigcap_{i\in J}\Ker p_{i}
,
so

h_{J}\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\Ker p_{i}=1
.
Therefore
h_{J}=1
,
whence

h=h^{\prime}\in\prod_{i\in J^{c}}L_{i}
.
This proves the claim.

Now let
J=I^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{j\}

be a subset of
I
.
Then

(\Ker p_{I^{\prime}})(\Ker p_{j})=(\prod_{i\notin I^{\prime}}L_{i})(\prod_{i\neq j}L_{i})=\prod_{i\in I}L_{i}=L=\Ker{\bar{p}}.

Hence (
3.3
) is semi-cartesian.
By Lemma
3.4
,

p_{I}
is surjective.
∎

We generalize
Definition
2.9
:

Definition 3.6
.

Assume that
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\in I}

are epimorphisms
and that
I\neq\emptyset
.
The fiber product

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is
compact
if
no proper closed subgroup of
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is mapped,
simultaneously for all
i\in I
,
by
\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}
onto
H_{i}
.

Obviously, if

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is compact and

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})

with
p_{i}
epimorphisms,
is in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
,
then the induced map

p_{I}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is surjective,
because the subgroup
p_{I}(H)
of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

cannot be proper.

Lemma 3.7
.

Assume that all the
\eta_{i}
are surjective.
Then

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is compact
if and only if

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is compact
for every finite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.

Proof.

Assume that
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact
and let
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.
Let
H^{\prime}\leq\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}

for every
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Let

H=\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}^{-1}(H^{\prime})\leq\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
We claim that

\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}(H)=H_{i}

for every
i\in I
.
Indeed,
if
i\in I^{\prime}
, then

\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}(H)=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}(\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}(H))=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}
.
If
i\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}
,
denote

E=\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}(H)
.
Then
E\leq H_{i}

and

\eta_{i}(E)=\eta_{I}(H)=\eta_{I^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})=G
.
Let
K_{i}
be as in Remark
3.2
(b),
then

K_{i}\leq H
,
hence

\Ker\eta_{i}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}(K_{i})\leq E
.
Thus
E=H_{i}
.

As
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact,

H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Hence

H^{\prime}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}(H)=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.

Now assume that
\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact
for every finite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.
We have

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\varprojlim_{I^{\prime}}\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Let
H^{\prime}\leq\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}

for every
i\in I
.
For every finite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I

put

H^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})\leq\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
then

H=\varprojlim_{I^{\prime}}H^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
.
But

\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}(H^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}})=\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}

for every
i\in I^{\prime}
,
hence

H^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

for every finite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.
Thus
H^{\prime}=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Assume that
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\in I}

are epimorphisms
and
I\neq\emptyset
.
Then

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is compact
if and only if
for every finite subset
I^{\prime}
of
I

and every
j\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}

the following cartesian square,
with
J=I^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{j\}
,
is compact.

\textstyle{\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{J,I^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{J,j}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{j}}
\textstyle{G}

(3.5)

Proof.

The square is cartesian
by Remark
3.2
(e).
By Lemma
3.7

we may assume that
I
is finite.

First assume that
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact
and consider diagram (
3.5
).
Let
H^{\prime}\leq\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\mathrm{pr}_{J,j}(H^{\prime})=H_{j}

and

\mathrm{pr}_{J,I^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Then

\mathrm{pr}_{J,i}(H^{\prime})=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}(\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i})=H_{i}

for every
i\in I^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
3.7
,

\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact,
hence

H^{\prime}=\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Thus (
3.5
) is compact.

Conversely,
assume that
(
3.5
) is compact,
for all
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
j\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}
.
We proceed by induction on
|I|
.

If
|I|=1
, say,
I=\{j\}
,
then
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=H_{j}
is compact.
So assume that

|I|\geq 2
.

Choose
j\in I

and
let
I^{\prime}=I\smallsetminus\{j\}
.
Put
J=I
.
As all subsets of
I^{\prime}
are also subsets of
I
,
by the induction hypothesis

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact.
Let
H^{\prime}\leq\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\mathrm{pr}_{J,i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}

for every
i\in J
.
Put
H^{\prime\prime}=\mathrm{pr}_{J,I^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})\leq\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Then
\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}(H^{\prime\prime})=\mathrm{pr}_{J,i}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}

for every
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Hence

H^{\prime\prime}=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Thus

\mathrm{pr}_{J,j}(H^{\prime})=H_{i}

and

\mathrm{pr}_{J,I^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
As
(
3.5
) is compact,

H^{\prime}=\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Thus
\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
I
be a set
and for every
i\in I
let
(
3.6
)
below
be a compact cartesian square.
Assume that
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact.

\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{i}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{i}}
\textstyle{A}

(3.6)

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\beta}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{A}}}{{\prod}_{A}}B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{I}}
\textstyle{A}

(3.7)

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\beta}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{A}}}{{\prod}_{A}}B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{I}}
\textstyle{A}

(3.8)

Then
the cartesian square
(
3.7
)
is compact.
Here

\eta_{I}

and

\alpha_{I}

are the structure maps of the fiber products
formed from

\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\in I}

and

\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
respectively,
and
\hat{\beta}
is the unique map
such that
(
3.8
)
above
commutes, for every
j\in I
.

Proof.

The existence and the uniqueness of
\hat{\beta}

follow from the universal property of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{A}}}{{\prod}_{A}}B_{i}
.
Using the explicit presentation (
3.1
) of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and a similar one of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{A}}}{{\prod}_{A}}B_{i}
,

\hat{\beta}
is

(h_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto(\beta_{i}(h_{i}))_{i\in I}
.
It easily follows that (
3.7
) is a cartesian square.
Therefore, as
\varphi
is surjective, so is
\hat{\beta}
.
As the
\eta_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}
are surjective,
so are

\eta_{I}
and
\alpha_{I}
.

To show that (
3.7
) is compact,
let
H^{\prime}\leq\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\hat{\beta}(H^{\prime})=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{A}}}{{\prod}_{A}}B_{i}

and

\eta_{I}(H^{\prime})=G
.
Put
H^{\prime}_{j}=\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(H^{\prime})\leq H_{j}
for every
j\in I
.
Then, from (
3.8
),

\beta_{j}(H^{\prime}_{j})=B_{j}

and

\eta_{j}(H^{\prime}_{j})=\eta_{I}(H^{\prime})=G
,
because
\eta_{j}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\eta_{I}
.
As (
3.6
) is compact,

H^{\prime}_{j}=H_{j}
.
Thus, as
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
is compact,

H^{\prime}=H
.
∎

4
A duality theory for
isotypic profinite semisimple modules

In this section
let
R
be a profinite ring
(
[
RZ
, Section 5.1]
)
and let
K
be
a
profinite
R
-module
,
that is,
a profinite abelian group on which
R
acts continuously.
Then
K
is an inverse limit of finite
R
-modules
(
[
RZ
, Lemma 5.1.1(b)]
).
Thus,
a submodule
L
of
K
is profinite
if and only if

L
is closed in
K
.

Fix a finite simple
R
-module
A
.
We denote

K^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Hom_{R}(K,A)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\varphi\colon K\to A|\,\varphi\textnormal{ is a continuous }R\textnormal{-homomorphism}\}

and
F=A^{*}=\Hom_{R}(A,A)=\End_{R}(A)
.
We say that
K
is
A
-generated
if

\bigcap_{\varphi\in K^{*}}\Ker\varphi=0.

Remark 4.1
.

(a)
Let
N=\bigcap_{\varphi\in K^{*}}\Ker\varphi
.
Then
K/N
is
A
-generated.
Indeed,
every
\varphi\in K^{*}
induces a unique
\varphi^{\prime}\in(K/N)^{*}
;
the map
\varphi\mapsto\varphi^{\prime}
is a bijection
K^{*}\to(K/N)^{*}

and
\Ker\varphi^{\prime}=(\Ker\varphi)/N
,
for every
\varphi\in K^{*}
.
Thus

\bigcap_{\varphi^{\prime}\in(K/N)^{*}}\Ker\varphi^{\prime}=\bigcap_{\varphi\in K^{*}}(\Ker\varphi)/N=(\bigcap_{\varphi\in K^{*}}\Ker\varphi)/N=N/N=0.

(b)
A closed
R
-submodule
C

of an
A
-generated
R
-module
K

is also
A
-generated.
Indeed,
every
\varphi\in K^{*}
restricts to an element of
C^{*}
,
and so

\bigcap_{\psi\in C^{*}}\Ker\psi\leq\bigcap_{\varphi\in K^{*}}(C\cap\Ker\varphi)\leq\bigcap_{\varphi\in K^{*}}\Ker\varphi=0.

(c)
Suppose that
K
is finite (as a set).
Then there is an open ideal
I
of
R

that acts trivially on
K
,
so that
K
is an
R/I
-module
(in the usual sense, without topological consideration).
Every submodule and every quotient module of
K

is also an
R/I
-module.
Thus, for a finite
K
,
the theory of semisimple modules applies.

(d)
If
K
is finite,
then
K^{*}
is finite,
say,
K^{*}=\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
and the map

x\mapsto(\varphi_{i}(x))_{i=1}^{n}

is an
R
-homomorphism

\pi\colon K\to\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
,
where
A_{i}\cong_{R}A
for each
i
.
As
\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
is isotypic semisimple,
so is
\pi(K)
, and hence

\pi(K)
is a direct sum of finitely many copies of
A
.
If
K
is also
A
-generated,

\pi
is injective,
hence
K
is
a direct sum of finitely many copies of
A
.

Example 4.2
.

Let
I
be a set and let

A^{I}
be the direct product of
|I|
copies of
A
,
that is,

A^{I}=\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
, where
A_{i}=A
for every
i\in I
.
(If
I=\emptyset
, then
A^{I}=0
.)
Then
A^{I}
is
A
-generated.
Indeed,
for every
i\in I
let
\varphi_{i}\colon A^{I}\twoheadrightarrow A=A_{i}

be the projection on the
i
-th coordinate.
This is an element of
(A^{I})^{*}
,
and
\bigcap_{i\in I}\Ker\varphi_{i}=0
.

Proposition
4.11

will show
that every
A
-generated
R
-module is of this form.

Remark 4.3
.

(a)
Let
\varphi\in K^{*}
.
Then
\varphi(K)
is an
R
-submodule of the simple
R
-module
A
,
hence either
\varphi=0
or
\varphi
is surjective.

(b)
By Schur’s Lemma,

F=\End_{R}(A)
is a division ring; as
A
is finite, so is
F
,
hence, by Wedderburn’s little theorem,

F
is a field.
In particular,
A
is a vector space over
F
:
here
F
acts on
A
by

\alpha a=\alpha(a)
,
for
\alpha\in F
and
a\in A
.
The actions of
R
and
F
on
A
commute.

(c)
The field
F
acts also on
K^{*}
, by

\alpha\varphi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\alpha\circ\varphi
,
for
\alpha\in F
and
\varphi\in K^{*}
.
It is easy to see that

K^{*}
is also a vector space over
F
.

(d)
A continuous homomorphism of
R
-modules

\beta\colon K\to K^{\prime}

defines an
F
-linear map

\beta^{*}\colon(K^{\prime})^{*}\to K^{*}
by

\varphi^{\prime}\mapsto\varphi^{\prime}\circ\beta
.
Thus
K\mapsto K^{*}
is a contravariant functor from
the category of profinite
R
-modules
to the category of vector spaces over
F
.
We restrict it to
A
-generated
R
-modules.

Dually to the definition of
K^{*}
for an
R
-module we have the following:

Definition 4.4
.

Let
V
be a vector space over
F
.
The set of maps
from
V
to
A

can be identified with the direct product
A^{V}
of copies of
A
,
and as such it is a profinite
R
-module in the product topology;
explicitly, the
R
-action is given by

(r\psi)(v)=r\psi(v)
,
where
r\in R
,
\psi\colon V\to A
, and
v\in V
.

This action is continuous:
First,
for every
a\in A
there is an open neighborhood
R_{a}
of
0
in
R

such that
R_{a}a=0
.
Then
R^{\prime}=\bigcap_{a\in A}R_{a}

is an open neighborhood of
0
in
R

such that
R^{\prime}A=0
.
Hence
R^{\prime}(A^{V})=0
.

Moreover,
A^{V}
is
A
-generated, by Example
4.2
.

The subset
V^{*}
of
A^{V}

consisting of
F
-linear transformations
V\to A

is clearly closed in
A^{V}
.
It is an
R
-module, since the actions of
R
and
F
on
A
commute.
As
A^{V}
is
A
-generated, so is
V^{*}
,
by Remark
4.1
(b).

Notice that if
C
is an
F
-linear basis of
V
,
then the restriction
A^{V}\twoheadrightarrow A^{C}
maps
V^{*}
isomorphically onto
A^{C}
.
Thus
V^{*}
is isomorphic to the direct product of

|C|=\dim_{F}V

copies of
A
.

An
F
-linear map

S\colon V^{\prime}\to V

of vector spaces over
F
defines
a continuous
R
-homomorphism

S^{*}\colon V^{*}\to(V^{\prime})^{*}
by

\psi\mapsto\psi\circ S
.
Thus
V\mapsto V^{*}
is a contravariant functor from
the category of vector spaces over
F

to the category of
A
-generated profinite
R
-modules.

Definition 4.5
.

If
K
is an
A
-generated
R
-module,
there is a natural homomorphism

\Theta_{K}\colon K\to(K^{*})^{*}
:
for every
b\in K
the linear transformation

\Theta_{K}(b)\colon K^{*}\to A

is given by
\varphi\mapsto\varphi(b)
.

Similarly,
if
V
is a vector space over
F
,
there is a natural homomorphism

\Lambda_{V}\colon V\to(V^{*})^{*}
:
for every
v\in V
the continuous
R
-homomorphism

\Lambda_{V}(v)\colon V^{*}\to A

is given by
\psi\mapsto\psi(v)
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\beta\colon K\to K^{\prime}
be a continuous homomorphism of

A
-generated
R
-modules.
Then the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{K^{**}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{**}}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{K^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{(K^{\prime})^{**}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Proof.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}\in(K^{\prime})^{*}

and
\psi\in K^{*}
.
Recall that

\beta^{*}(\varphi^{\prime})=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\beta

and

\beta^{**}(\psi)=\psi\circ\beta^{*}
,
hence

\big(\beta^{**}(\psi)\big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\big(\psi\circ\beta^{*}\big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\psi\big(\beta^{*}(\varphi^{\prime})\big)=\psi(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\beta).

(4.1)

Let
b\in K
.
By the definition of
\Theta_{K^{\prime}}
we have

\big((\Theta_{K^{\prime}}\circ\beta)(b)\big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\Big(\Theta_{K^{\prime}}\big(\beta(b)\big)\Big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\varphi^{\prime}\big(\beta(b)\big)=(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\beta)(b)

and on the other hand by (
4.1
)

\big((\beta^{**}\circ\Theta_{K})(b)\big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\Big(\beta^{**}\big(\Theta_{K}(b)\big)\Big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\big(\Theta_{K}(b)\big)(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\beta)=(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\beta)(b).

Thus

\big((\Theta_{K^{\prime}}\circ\beta)(b)\big)(\varphi^{\prime})=\big((\beta^{**}\circ\Theta_{K})(b)\big)(\varphi^{\prime})

for every
\varphi^{\prime}\in(K^{\prime})^{*}
.
∎

Completely analogously we have:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
S\colon V\to V^{\prime}
be an
F
-linear map of
vector spaces over
F
.
Then the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{V}}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{V^{**}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{**}}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{V^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{(V^{\prime})^{**}}

Proof.

Let
\psi^{\prime}\in(V^{\prime})^{*}

and
\varphi\in V^{*}
.
Recall that

S^{*}(\psi^{\prime})=\psi^{\prime}\circ S

and

S^{**}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ S^{*}
,
hence

\big(S^{**}(\varphi)\big)(\psi^{\prime})=\big(\varphi\circ S^{*}\big)(\psi^{\prime})=\varphi\big(S^{*}(\psi^{\prime})\big)=\varphi(\psi^{\prime}\circ S\big).

(4.2)

Let
v\in V
.
By the definition of
\Lambda_{V^{\prime}}
we have

\big(\Lambda_{V^{\prime}}\circ S(v)\big)(\psi^{\prime})=\Big(\Lambda_{V^{\prime}}\big(S(v)\big)\Big)(\psi^{\prime})=\psi^{\prime}\big(S(v)\big)=(\psi^{\prime}\circ S)(v)

and on the other hand by (
4.2
)

\big((S^{**}\circ\Lambda_{V})(v)\big)(\psi^{\prime})=\Big(S^{**}\big(\Lambda_{V}(v)\big)\Big)(\psi^{\prime})=\big(\Lambda_{V}(v)\big)(\psi^{\prime}\circ S)=(\psi^{\prime}\circ S)(v).

Thus

\big(\Lambda_{V^{\prime}}\circ S(v)\big)(\psi^{\prime})=\big((S^{**}\circ\Lambda_{V})(v)\big)(\psi^{\prime})

for every
\psi^{\prime}\in(V^{\prime})^{*}
.
∎

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
K
be an
A
-generated profinite
R
-module
and let
V
be a vector space over
F
.
Then

\Theta_{K}\colon K\to(K^{*})^{*}

and

\Lambda_{V}\colon V\to(V^{*})^{*}

are isomorphisms.

The
-^{*}
functors establish an equivalence
between the categories of
A
-generated
R
-modules and
vector spaces over
F
.

We need some auxiliary results to prove the theorem.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
K=A^{I}
and for every
i\in I

let
\varphi_{i}\colon K\twoheadrightarrow A

be the projection on the
i
-th coordinate.
Then
\{\varphi_{i}|\,i\in I\}
is a basis of
K^{*}
over
F
.

Proof.

We first show that the set is linearly independent over
F
.
Let
\alpha_{i}\in F
,
almost all zero,
such that
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\varphi_{i}=0
.
Let
j\in I
and let
a\in A
.
Define
b\in K
by

\varphi_{j}(b)=a
and
\varphi_{i}(b)=0
for
i\neq j
.
Then

0=\left(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\varphi_{i}\right)(b)=\alpha_{j}(a)
.
Hence
\alpha_{j}=0
.

Now we show that

\left(\varphi_{i}\right)_{i\in I}

spans
K^{*}
.
Write
K
as
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
,
where
A_{j}=A
for every
j\in I
;
explicitly,

A_{j}=\bigcap_{i\neq j}\Ker\varphi_{i}
.
Let
\psi\colon K\to A
be an element of
K^{*}
.
For every
i\in I

let
\alpha_{i}\colon A\to A

be the restriction of
\psi
to
A_{i}
.
This is an
R
-homomorphism and hence
\alpha_{i}\in F
.
As
\psi
is continuous,
\Ker\psi
is open in
K
,
and hence contains every
A_{i}
,
except for
i
in a finite subset
I^{\prime}
of
I
.
Thus
\alpha_{i}=0
for all
i\notin I^{\prime}
.
We claim that

\psi=\sum_{j\in I}\alpha_{j}\varphi_{j}
,
that is,

\psi=\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}\varphi_{j}
.

Indeed,
let
b\in K
.
Let
a_{i}
be its
i
th coordinate.
Then
b=b^{\prime}+\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}b_{j}
, where

(b^{\prime})_{i}=\begin{cases}0&\textnormal{if }i\in I^{\prime}\\
a_{i}&\textnormal{if }i\notin I^{\prime}\\
\end{cases}

and

(b_{j})_{i}=\begin{cases}a_{i}&\textnormal{if }i=j\\
0&\textnormal{otherwise }\\
\end{cases}
.
Hence

\psi(b)=\psi(b^{\prime})+\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\psi(b_{j})=0+\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}(a_{j})=\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}(\varphi_{j}(b))=\left(\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}\varphi_{j}\right)(b),

whence

\psi=\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}\varphi_{j}=\sum_{j\in I}\alpha_{j}\varphi_{j}
.
∎

We will need a version of
the Chinese Remainder Theorem:

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\in K^{*}
be linearly independent over
F
.
Then the
R
-homomorphism

K\to A^{n}

given by

b\mapsto(\varphi_{1}(b),\ldots,\varphi_{n}(b))

is surjective.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.
For every
i
let
N_{i}=\Ker\varphi_{i}
.

By induction hypothesis

K\to\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}K/N_{i}

is surjective.
Its kernel is

N\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}N_{i}
,
so
K/N\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}K/N_{i}
is an isomorphism.
Therefore so is

K/N\times K/N_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{n}K/N_{i}
.
As
K\to\prod_{i=1}^{n}K/N_{i}
is the composition of

K\to K/N\times K/N_{n}

and
the latter map,
we have to show that

K\to K/N\times K/N_{n}

is surjective,
that is, that

N+N_{n}=K
.

Let
{\bar{K}}=K/N

and let
\bar{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\varphi}_{n-1}\colon{\bar{K}}\to A

be the maps induced from
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n-1}
.
The above isomorphism

{\bar{K}}\to\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}K/N_{i}

gives
{\bar{K}}
the structure of the direct product
A^{n-1}

such that

\bar{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\varphi}_{n-1}
are the coordinate projections.
By Lemma
4.9
,

\bar{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\varphi}_{n-1}
are a basis of
{\bar{K}}^{*}
.
Therefore,
if
N\subseteq N_{n}
,
the map
\bar{\varphi}_{n}\colon{\bar{K}}\to A
induced from
\varphi_{n}

is a linear combination of
\bar{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\varphi}_{n-1}
,
say,

\bar{\varphi}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n=1}\alpha_{i}\bar{\varphi}_{i}
.
It follows that

\varphi_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n=1}\alpha_{i}\varphi_{i}
,
a contradiction.
Thus,

N\not\subseteq N_{n}
.
Hence

N_{n}\subsetneqq N+N_{n}
.

As
A=K/N_{n}
is a simple
R
-module,
there is no
R
-submodule
M
of
K

such that

N_{n}\subsetneqq M\subsetneqq K
.
By assumption

N_{n}\subsetneqq N+N_{n}\subseteq K
,
hence
N+N_{n}=K
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
K
be an
A
-generated
R
-module.
Let
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a basis of
K^{*}
as a vector space over
F
.
Then

\varphi\colon K\to A^{I}

given by

\varphi(b)=\left(\varphi_{i}(b)\right)_{i\in I}

is an isomorphism of
R
-modules.

Proof.

Clearly
\varphi
is an abstract
R
-homomorphism.

It is continuous:
A basic open neighborhood of
0
in
A^{I}

is of the form

\{(a_{i})_{i\in I}|\,a_{i}=0\text{ for all }i\in I^{\prime}\}
,
for some finite subset
I^{\prime}
of
I
,
and its inverse image under
\varphi

is the open subset

\bigcap_{i\in I^{\prime}}\Ker\varphi_{i}
.

We show that
\varphi
is injective.
Let
b\in K
such that
\varphi(b)=0
.
Then
\varphi_{i}(b)=0
for every
i\in I
.
As every
\rho\in K^{*}

is a linear combination of finitely many
\varphi_{i}
’s,
we have
\rho(b)=0
for every
\rho\in K^{*}
.
Therefore, since
K
is
A
-generated,

b\in\bigcap_{\rho\in K^{*}}\Ker\rho=\{0\}
,
that is,
b=0
.

If
I^{\prime}
is finite subset of
I
,
the induced map

\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\circ\varphi\colon K\to A^{I^{\prime}}

is surjective
by Lemma
4.10
.
As

A^{I}=\varprojlim\limits_{I^{\prime}\subseteq I\textnormal{ finite}}A^{I^{\prime}}
,
where the structure maps
A^{I}\to A^{I^{\prime}}

are the restrictions from
I
to
I^{\prime}
,
the assertion follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.8
.

(a)
We first show that
\Theta_{K}
is an isomorphism.

By Proposition
4.11

we may assume that
K=A^{I}
for some set
I
;
By Lemma
4.9

the coordinate projections

(\varphi_{i}\colon K\twoheadrightarrow A)_{i\in I}
are a basis of
K^{*}
.
As noted in Definition
4.4
,

(K^{*})^{*}
is the set of all functions from this basis to
A
;
hence it can be identified with
A^{I}
.

Under this identification
\Theta_{K}
is the identity.
Indeed,
let
b=(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in K
.
Then
\Theta_{K}(b)
maps
\varphi_{i}
onto
\varphi_{i}(b)=a_{i}
,
hence it is identified with the map
I\to A

that maps
i
onto
a_{i}
, that is, with
b
.

We now show that
\Lambda_{V}
is an isomorphism.

Let
C
be a basis of
V
.
By Definition
4.4
,

V^{*}=A^{C}
.
The coordinate projections of
A^{C}
can be identified with the elements
of
C
(a projection
\varphi
is identified with the unique
v\in C

such that
\varphi(v)\neq 0
).
By Lemma
4.9

(V^{*})^{*}=(A^{C})^{*}

is the vector space with basis consisting of these coordinate projections,
that is, it can be identified with a vector space with basis
C
,
that is, with
V
.

Under this identification
\Lambda_{V}
is the identity on
C

and hence also on
V
.
Indeed,
let
v\in C
.
Then
\Lambda_{V}(v)
is the projection
\varphi\colon A^{C}\twoheadrightarrow A

that maps
(a_{u})_{u\in C}
to
a_{v}
,
hence it is identified with
v
.

By Lemmas
4.6

and
4.7
,

\Theta_{K}
and
\Lambda_{V}
are natural isomorphisms between
the composition of the
-^{*}
functors and the identity functors.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

Let

T\colon V\to W

be an
F
-linear map of vector spaces over
F
.
By linear algebra we know that

(a)

T^{*}\colon W^{*}\to V^{*}
is
injective
if and only if

T
is surjective;

(b)

T^{*}\colon W^{*}\to V^{*}
is
surjective
if and only if

T
is injective.

It follows by the duality that
if

\alpha\colon K\to C
is a continuous homomorphism
of
A
-generated
R
-modules, then

(c)

\alpha^{*}\colon C^{*}\to K^{*}
is
surjective
if and only if

\alpha
is injective;

(d)

\alpha^{*}\colon C^{*}\to K^{*}
is
injective
if and only if

\alpha
is surjective.

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
L
be a closed submodule of
an
A
-generated
R
-module
K
.
Then there is
an
A
-generated
R
-module
M

such that

K=L\oplus M
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\colon L\to K
be the inclusion map.
By Remark
4.12
(c),

\alpha^{*}\colon K^{*}\to L^{*}
is surjective.
Let
V=\Ker\alpha^{*}
.
By linear algebra
we may view
L^{*}

as an
F
-subspace of
K^{*}

such that

K^{*}=L^{*}\oplus V

and
\alpha^{*}
is the identity on
L^{*}
.
By Theorem
4.8
,

K=L\oplus V^{*}
,
where

V^{*}
is
A
-generated.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

A quotient module of
an
A
-generated
R
-module
is
A
-generated.

Proof.

Let
K\twoheadrightarrow M

be an epimorphisms of
R
-modules,
such that

K
is
A
-generated.
Let
L
be its kernel.
By Corollary
4.13
,
there is
an
A
-generated
R
-module
M^{\prime}

such that

K=L\oplus M^{\prime}
.
Thus
M\cong K/L\cong M^{\prime}

is
A
-generated.
∎

Corollary 4.15
.

Let
K
be an
A
-generated
R
-module
and let
L
be a closed submodule of
K
.
Then we can choose a basis

\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
K^{*}

such that the isomorphism

\varphi\colon K\to A^{I}

of Proposition
4.11

defined by

\varphi(b)=\left(\varphi_{i}(b)\right)_{i\in I}

maps
L
onto

A^{J}
for some subset
J
of
I
.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.13

there is an
A
-generated
R
-module
M

such that

K=L\oplus M
.
Choose a basis

\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in J}
of
L^{*}

and extend every element of it to an element of
K^{*}

by the zero on
M
.
Analogously
choose a basis

\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in J^{\prime}}
of
M^{*}

and extend every element of it to an element of
K^{*}

by the zero on
L
.
Put
I=J\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}J^{\prime}
.
Then

\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in I}

is a basis of
K^{*}
.
By Proposition
4.11
,
applied to
L
instead of
K
,
\varphi
maps
L
onto
A^{J}
.
∎

5
A duality theory for extensions by
isotypic profinite semisimple modules

We consider the cohomology of a profinite group
G

with coefficients in profinite modules
(i.e., objects in the category
\mathcal{C}_{R}(G)

in
[
Sy
, Section 2.6]
).

A homomorphism of profinite
G
-modules

\beta\colon K\to L

induces a map
\beta_{*}\colon H^{2}(G,K)\to H^{2}(G,L)
.
By the correspondence between the second cohomology and group extensions
(
[
RZ
, p. 233]
)
it induces a map between extensions of
G
by
K

to extensions of
G
by
L
.
It is an exercise
to give the following
explicit description of the latter map:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
G
be a profinite group
and let

\beta\colon K\to L

be a homomorphism of profinite
G
-modules.
Let

\mathcal{H}\colon\ \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 12.60417pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.60417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.60417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.60417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.85344pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.72914pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 75.72914pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathcal{E}\colon\ \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 11.4028pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.4028pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.4028pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.4028pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.49901pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.36111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.36111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(5.1)

be two extensions.
Then
\mathcal{E}=\beta_{*}(\mathcal{H})

if and only if
there exists a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
