\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

(5.2)

Moreover, if such a diagram exists,

\varepsilon
is unique up to a composition with an automorphism

\omega
of
E
such that
\pi\circ\omega=\pi

and
\omega|_{L}=\mathrm{id}_{L}
.

If
\beta
is surjective/isomorphism, then so is
\varepsilon
.

Recall that
extensions (
5.1
)
are
congruent

(
[
Ri
, p. 100]
),
if there is a commutative diagram
(
5.2
)
with
K=L
and
\beta=\mathrm{id}_{K}
.
Thus, given
\mathcal{H}
,
an extension
\mathcal{E}
such that
(
5.2
)
commutes
is unique up to a congruence of extensions.
We identify congruent extensions,
and thus
\mathcal{E}
is unique.

We will also have to classify extensions up to isomorphism:

Extensions (
5.1
)
– even with non-abelian kernels
K,L
–
are
isomorphic
,
if there is an isomorphism of profinite groups

\varepsilon\colon H\to E

such that

\pi\circ\varepsilon=\eta
.
If this is the case
and
K,L
are abelian
G
-modules,
then

\beta:=\varepsilon|_{K}\colon K\to L

is an isomorphism of profinite
G
-modules
such that (
5.2
)
commutes;
thus we may assume that
L=K

and
\beta\in\Aut_{G}(K)
,
and

\mathcal{E}=\beta_{*}(\mathcal{H})
.
Conversely, by Lemma
5.1
,
every
\beta\in\Aut_{G}(K)
defines
an extension isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}
,
namely,

\beta_{*}(\mathcal{H})
.
So,

\Aut_{G}(K)
acts on
H^{2}(G,K)

and
the isomorphism classes of extensions of
G

by
K

bijectively correspond to the elements of

H^{2}(G,K)/\Aut_{G}(K)
.

Since we deal with commutative diagrams with epimorphisms
of profinite groups,
it will be convenient to redefine the inflation map as follows:
Let
\varphi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{G}}
be an epimorphism
of profinite groups.
A profinite
{\bar{G}}
-module
K
is also a
G
-module –

G
acts on
K
by composing
\varphi
with the
{\bar{G}}
-action on
K
.
The
inflation along
\varphi

is the map

\Inf_{\varphi}\colon H^{2}({\bar{G}},K)\to H^{2}(G,K)

induced from the map on cochains
given by composing the
{\bar{G}}
-cochains with
\varphi
.
It is an exercise to show that:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
{\bar{G}}
be a profinite group
and
K
a profinite
{\bar{G}}
-module.
Let
\varphi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{G}}

and let
G
act on
K
via
\varphi
.
Let

\alpha\colon B\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{G}}

and

\eta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be extensions with kernel
K

(with the above action).
Then

\eta
is isomorphic to the inflation
\Inf_{\varphi}(\alpha)

of
\alpha
along
\varphi

if and only if
there exists a cartesian square

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\bar{G}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

(5.3)

Following this we write

\eta\cong_{G}\Inf_{\varphi}(\alpha)

if there is a cartesian square (
5.3
),
even if
\Ker\alpha,\Ker\eta
are not abelian.

Corollary 5.3
.

The following two categories are equivalent:

(a)

The class of pairs
(K,f)
,
where
K
is a profinite
G
-module
and
f\in H^{2}(G,K)
;
morphism
(K,f)\to(L,g)
in this category
is a
G
-homomorphism
\beta\colon K\to L

such that
g=\beta_{*}f=\beta\circ f
.

(b)

The class of profinite group extensions

0\to K\to H\to G\to 1
,
up to congruence,
where
K
is a profinite
G
-module;
morphism in this category
is a commutative diagram (
5.2
).

For the rest of this section fix a

finite simple
G
-module
A
. Thus
A
is a finite simple
[[\mathbb{Z}G]]
-module,
and so we may apply the material of section
4

with
R=[[\mathbb{Z}G]]
.
Recall (Remark
4.3
(b))
that
F=\End_{G}(A)
is a finite field.

Remark 5.4
.

We use the notation of
[
Ri
, Chapter II §1]
.

(a)
Notice that the

group of non-homogeneous cochains

{\bar{C}}^{n}(G,A)

is a vector space over
F
:
For every
\alpha\in F
we have

\alpha f:=\alpha\circ f
.
Moreover,
the boundary operator

\bar{\partial}\colon{\bar{C}}^{n}(G,A)\to{\bar{C}}^{n+1}(G,A)

commutes with
\alpha
,
hence it is an
F
-linear operator.

It follows that

{\bar{B}}^{n}(G,A)\subseteq{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)

are vector subspaces of
{\bar{C}}^{n}(G,A)
.
Thus

H^{n}(G,A)={\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)/{\bar{B}}^{n}(G,A)

is a vector space over
F
.

(b)
In particular,
by Corollary
5.3
,
the set of congruence classes
of epimorphisms onto
G

with kernel
A
(as a
G
-module)
is also a vector space over
F
.
By Lemma
1.6
(c),
these epimorphisms are necessarily indecomposable.
In this sense we speak below
of linear combinations
of indecomposable epimorphisms onto
G
with kernel
A
.

(c)
If
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
is an epimorphism of profinite groups,
then
A
is also a simple
H
-module
and
\End_{H}(A)=\End_{G}(A)=F
.
It is easy to see that the
inflation

\Inf_{\pi}\colon H^{n}(G,A)\to H^{n}(H,A)

is an
F
-linear map.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let

(H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G|\,i\in I)

be a family of extensions with kernel
A
,
as a
G
-module.
For every
i\in I

let
f_{i}\in{\bar{Z}}^{2}(G,A)

be a (non-homogeneous) cochain representing
H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
.
Then

\eta_{I}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G

is represented by a cochain

g\in{\bar{Z}}^{2}(G,A^{I})

given by

g(\sigma,\tau)=\big(f_{i}(\sigma,\tau)\big)_{i\in I}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in I
.
We think of
f_{i}
as a continuous function

f_{i}\colon G\times G\to A

with
\bar{\partial}f_{i}=0
.
Thus
there is a continuous section
u_{i}\colon G\to H_{i}

of
H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
such that

u_{i}(\sigma)u_{i}(\tau)=f_{i}(\sigma,\tau)u_{i}(\sigma\tau),\qquad\sigma,\tau\in G.

Now,

\sigma\mapsto\big(u_{i}(\sigma)\big)_{i\in I}

is a continuous section

u\colon G\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
of
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
,
and the map
g\colon G\times G\to A^{I}

given by

g(\sigma,\tau)=\big(f_{i}(\sigma,\tau)\big)_{i\in I}

is continuous and satisfies

u(\sigma)u(\tau)=g(\sigma,\tau)u(\sigma\tau),\qquad\sigma,\tau\in G.

Hence
g
is a cochain
and it represents
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
.
∎

Definition 5.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We will consider the following two categories:

(a)

The class
\mathcal{H}^{n}
of pairs
(K,f)
,
where
K
is a profinite
A
-generated
G
-module
and
f\in H^{n}(G,K)
;
morphism
(K_{1},f_{1})\to(K_{2},f_{2})
in this category
is a
G
-homomorphism
\beta\colon K_{1}\to K_{2}

such that
f_{2}=\beta_{*}f_{1}=\beta\circ f_{1}
.

(b)

The class
\mathcal{T}^{n}
of pairs
(V,S)
,
where
V
is a vector space over
F
and

S\colon V\to H^{n}(G,A)
is an
F
-linear transformation;
morphism
(V_{1},S_{1})\to(V_{2},S_{2})
in this category
is
an
F
-linear map
T\colon V_{2}\to V_{1}
such that

S_{2}=S_{1}\circ T
.

In particular, if
n=2
,
then (a) is equivalent to

(a’)

The class of profinite group extensions

0\to K\to H\to G\to 1
,
up to congruence,
where
K
is a profinite
A
-generated
G
-module;
morphism in this category
is a commutative diagram (
5.2
).

Construction 5.7
.

We construct two contravariant functors,

X^{n}\colon\mathcal{H}^{n}\to\mathcal{T}^{n}
and
Y^{n}\colon\mathcal{T}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}^{n}
,
which give an equivalence of categories.

(a)

Construction of

X^{n}\colon\mathcal{H}^{n}\to\mathcal{T}^{n}
.

Let
(K,f)\in\mathcal{H}^{n}
.
By Remark
4.3
(c),

K^{*}=\Hom_{G}(K,A)
is a vector space over
F
.
A
G
-homomorphism

\varphi\colon K\to A

defines a map

\varphi_{*}\colon{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K)\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)
by

f^{\prime}\mapsto\varphi\circ f^{\prime}
.
Thus a representative
f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K)
of
f
defines a map

S_{K,f^{\prime}}\colon K^{*}\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)

by

S_{K,f^{\prime}}(\varphi)=\varphi_{*}(f^{\prime})=\varphi\circ f^{\prime}
.
It is easy to see that

S_{K,f^{\prime}}
is an
F
-linear transformation.
Hence it induces an
F
-linear transformation

S_{K,f}\colon K^{*}\to H^{n}(G,A).

(5.4)

Notice that
S_{K,f}
does not depend on the choice of
f^{\prime}

(and hence we may write
S_{K,f}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ f
):
If
f^{\prime}\sim f^{\prime\prime}
, that is,
f^{\prime}=f^{\prime\prime}+\bar{\partial}g

for some
g\in{\bar{C}}^{n-1}(G,K)
,
then

\varphi\circ f^{\prime}=\varphi\circ f^{\prime\prime}+\varphi\circ\bar{\partial}g,\quad\textnormal{ where }\varphi\circ\bar{\partial}g=\bar{\partial}(\varphi\circ g)\in{\bar{B}}^{n}(G,K),

hence
f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}
define the same map

S_{K,f}\colon K^{*}\to H^{n}(G,A)
.
Put
X^{n}(K,f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(K^{*},S_{K,f})
.

We remark that if
n=2

and we identify
(K,f)
with an extension
\eta
of
G
by
K
,
by Corollary
5.3
,
then

S_{K,f}(\varphi)
is the extension
\pi
of
G
by
A

such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

(5.5)

i.e.,

\Img S_{K,f}

consists of all indecomposable extensions of
G

dominated by
\eta
.

Let
\beta\colon(K_{1},f_{1})\to(K_{2},f_{2})

be a morphism in
\mathcal{H}^{n}
.
The
G
-homomorphism
\beta\colon K_{1}\to K_{2}

induces an
F
-linear map

\beta^{*}\colon K_{2}^{*}\to K_{1}^{*}

by
\psi\mapsto\psi\circ\beta
.
We claim that

S_{K_{2},f_{2}}=S_{K_{1},f_{1}}\circ\beta^{*}
.
Indeed,
let
\psi\in K_{2}^{*}
.
Let
f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K_{1})
be a representative of
f_{1}\in H^{n}(G,K_{1})
.
Then
\beta\circ f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K_{2})
is a representative of

f_{2}=\beta_{*}(f_{1})\in H^{n}(G,K_{2})
.
Therefore

S_{K_{1},f^{\prime}}\circ\beta^{*}(\psi)=S_{K_{1},f^{\prime}}(\psi\circ\beta)=\psi\circ\beta\circ f^{\prime}=S_{K_{2},\beta\circ f^{\prime}}(\psi)=S_{K_{2},\beta_{*}(f^{\prime})}(\psi).

Modulo
{\bar{B}}^{n}(G,K)

this gives

S_{K_{1},f_{1}}\circ\beta^{*}(\psi)=S_{K_{2},f_{2}}(\psi)
.

Thus
\beta^{*}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{T}^{n}
.
Put
X^{n}(\beta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\beta^{*}
.

This completes the definition of
X^{n}\colon\mathcal{H}^{n}\to\mathcal{T}^{n}
.

(b)

Construction of
Y^{n}\colon\mathcal{T}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}^{n}
.

Let
(V,S)\in\mathcal{T}^{n}
.
Lift
S\colon V\to H^{n}(G,A)
to an
F
-linear transformation

S^{\prime}\colon V\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)
.
Thus, for every
v\in V
, the map

S^{\prime}(v)\colon G^{n}\to A

is continuous, i.e., locally constant, and satisfies

0=\Big(\bar{\partial}\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)\Big)(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n+1})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{1}\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\ldots,\sigma_{n+1})+\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n+1})+(-1)^{n}\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n}).

Recall
(Definition
4.4
)
that
V^{*}
is a profinite
G
-module.
Define a map

f^{\prime}\colon G^{n}\to V^{*}

by

\big(f^{\prime}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})\big)(v)=\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})
.

It is continuous:
A basic open subset
V^{*}
is of the form

R=\{{\varphi\in V^{*}}|\,S^{\prime}(v_{i})=a_{i},\ i=1,\ldots,n\},\text{ for some }\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq V,\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq A.

Then

(f^{\prime})^{-1}(R)=\{\sigma\in G^{n}|\,S^{\prime}(v_{i})(\sigma)=a_{i},\ i=1,\ldots,n\}

is open in
G^{n}
,
because
S^{\prime}(v_{i})
is continuous for every
i
.

It is easy to check that

\big((\bar{\partial}f^{\prime})(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n+1})\big)(v)=\big(\bar{\partial}\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)\big)(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n+1}),

hence, as
\bar{\partial}\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)=0
, we have

f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,V^{*})
.
Thus
f^{\prime}
defines an element
f\in H^{n}(G,V^{*})
,
the class of
f^{\prime}
.
Put
Y^{n}(V,S)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(V^{*},f)
.

Notice that
f
does not depend on the choice of
S^{\prime}
:
If
S^{\prime\prime}\colon V\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)
is another lifting of
S
,
it defines
f^{\prime\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,V^{*})
.
As
S^{\prime}-S^{\prime\prime}\colon V\to{\bar{B}}^{n}(G,A)
,
for every
v\in V
there is

g_{v}\in{\bar{C}}^{n-1}(G,A)
such that

S^{\prime}(v)-S^{\prime\prime}(v)=\bar{\partial}g_{v}
.
Define
g\in{\bar{C}}^{n-1}(G,V^{*})
by

\big(g(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1})\big)(v)=\big(g_{v}\big)(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1})
.
Then

\big((f^{\prime}-f^{\prime\prime})(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})\big)(v)=\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})-\big(S^{\prime\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})=\\
=\big(\bar{\partial}g_{v}\big)(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})=\sigma_{1}g_{v}(\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n})+\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}g_{v}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})+(-1)^{n}g_{v}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})=\\
\sigma_{1}g(\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n})(v)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}g(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})(v)+\\
(-1)^{n}gv(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})(v)=\big(\bar{\partial}g(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})\big)(v),

hence
f^{\prime}-f^{\prime\prime}=\bar{\partial}g\in{\bar{B}}^{n}(G,V^{*})
,
whence
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}
define the same element
f\in H^{n}(G,V^{*})
.

Let
T\colon(V_{1},S_{1})\to(V_{2},S_{2})

be a morphism in
\mathcal{T}^{n}
.
Then
T\colon V_{2}\to V_{1}
is an
F
-linear map
(that induces a continuous
G
-homomorphism

T^{*}\colon V_{1}^{*}\to V_{2}^{*}

by
T^{*}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ T
)
and
S_{2}=S_{1}\circ T
.
Lift
S_{1}
to an
F
-linear map
S^{\prime}_{1}\colon V_{1}\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)
,
then
S^{\prime}_{2}=S^{\prime}_{1}\circ T
lifts
S_{2}
.
For
i=1,2

let
(V_{i}^{*},f_{i})=Y^{n}(V_{i},S_{i})
and let
f_{i}^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)

be a representative of
f_{i}
, defined by

\big(f_{i}^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)(v)=\big(S^{\prime}_{i}(v)\big)(\sigma)
,
for all
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})\in G^{n}
and
v\in V_{i}
.

Let
\sigma\in G^{n+1}
and
v\in V_{2}
. Then

\big(S^{\prime}_{2}(v)\big)(\sigma)=\big(S^{\prime}_{1}(T(v))\big)(\sigma)
,
hence

\big(f_{2}^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)(v)=\big(f_{1}^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)\big(T(v)\big)=\Big(T^{*}\big(f_{1}^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)\Big)(v).

This is true for every
v\in V_{2}
,
so

f_{2}^{\prime}(\sigma)=T^{*}\big(f_{1}^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)

for every
\sigma
,
whence
f_{2}^{\prime}=T\circ f_{1}^{\prime}
.
Thus
T^{*}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{H}^{n}
.
Put
Y^{n}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=T^{*}
.

This completes the definition of
Y^{n}\colon\mathcal{T}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}^{n}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

The categories
\mathcal{T}^{n}
and
\mathcal{H}^{n}
are dually equivalent,
via the functors
X^{n}
and
Y^{n}
.

Proof.

We use the natural isomorphisms defined in
Definition
4.5
.

Let
(K,f)\in\mathcal{H}^{n}
,
let
(V,S)=X^{n}(K,f)
,
and put
(C,g)=Y^{n}(V,S)
.
Then
C=\Theta_{K}(V)
and
g=\Theta_{K}\circ f
.
Indeed,

V=K^{*}
and
C=V^{*}=K^{**}
;
by Theorem
4.8
,
K^{**}=\Theta_{K}(K)
.
Let
f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K)
lift
f
.
Then
S
is induced from
S^{\prime}\colon K^{*}\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)

given by

S^{\prime}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ f^{\prime}

and
g
is induced from
g^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,V^{*})
given by

\big(g^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)(\varphi)=\big(S^{\prime}(\varphi)\big)(\sigma)

for every
\varphi\in V=K^{*}
and every
\sigma\in G^{n+1}
.
Hence

\big(g^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)(\varphi)=\big(S^{\prime}(\varphi)\big)(\sigma)=(\varphi\circ f^{\prime})(\sigma)=\varphi\big(f^{\prime}(\sigma)\big).

On the other hand,
by the definition of
\Theta_{K}
,

[(\Theta_{K}\circ f^{\prime})(\sigma)](\varphi)=\big[\Theta_{K}\big(f^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)\big](\varphi)=\varphi\big(f^{\prime}(\sigma)\big).

Hence

g^{\prime}=\Theta_{K}\circ f^{\prime}
.
It follows that

g=\Theta_{K}\circ f
.

Conversely,
let
(V,S)\in\mathcal{T}^{n}
,
let
(K,f)=Y^{n}(V,S)
,
and
(W,R)=X^{n}(K,f)
.
Then
W=\Lambda_{V}(V)
and
R\circ\Lambda_{V}=S
.
Indeed,

K=V^{*}
and
W=K^{*}=V^{**}
;
by Theorem
4.8
,
V^{**}=\Lambda_{V}(V)
.
Lift
S
to an
F
-linear map
S^{\prime}\colon V\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)
.
Then
f
is induced from
f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,V^{*})
given by

f^{\prime}(\sigma)(v)=\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma)
,
and
R
is induced from
R^{\prime}\colon K^{*}\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,A)
given by

R^{\prime}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ f^{\prime}
.
Let
v\in V
and fix
\sigma\in G^{n+1}
.
Then

R^{\prime}\big(\Lambda_{V}(v)\big)=\Lambda_{V}(v)\circ f^{\prime}
,
hence

R^{\prime}\big(\Lambda_{V}(v)\big)(\sigma)=\big(\Lambda_{V}(v)\circ f^{\prime}\big)(\sigma)=\Lambda_{V}(v)\big(f^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)=\big(f^{\prime}(\sigma)\big)(v)=\big(S^{\prime}(v)\big)(\sigma),

whence

R^{\prime}\circ\Lambda_{V}(v)=S^{\prime}(v)
.
It follows that

R\circ\Lambda_{V}(v)=S(v)
.
∎

Example 5.9
.

Let
I
be a set.
For every
i\in I
let

\textstyle{0\to A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{i}}
\textstyle{G\to 1}

(5.6)

be an extension of
G
by the simple
G
-module
A

and let

f_{i}\in H^{2}(G,A)

be the corresponding cocycle.
Let

\textstyle{0\to K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{G\to 1}

(5.7)

be the fiber product extension
and let

f\in H^{2}(G,A^{I})

be the corresponding cocycle.
Then
K=A^{I}
and
(K,f)\in\mathcal{H}^{2}
.
What is
(K^{*},S_{K,f})=X^{2}(A^{I},f)
?

Let
\varphi_{i}\colon K\twoheadrightarrow A

be the projection on the
i
-th coordinate.
By Lemma
4.9
,

K^{*}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}F\varphi_{i}
.
By Lemma
5.5
,

f
is given by

\varphi_{i}\circ f=f_{i}
, for every
i\in I
.
By Construction
5.7
(a),

S_{K,f}(\varphi_{i})=f_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
.
This completely determines

(K^{*},S_{K,f})
,
up to isomorphism.

By elementary linear algebra

\dim K^{*}=|I|

and

\dim\Ker S_{K,f}=|I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}|
,
where

I^{\prime}
is a subset of
I

such that

(f_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}

is a maximal linearly independent subset of

(f_{i})_{i\in I}
.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\eta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be an epimorphism
such that
K:=\ker\eta

is an
A
-generated module.
Let
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a basis of
K^{*}
.
Denote
f_{i}=S(\psi_{i})\in H^{2}(G,A)

and let (
5.6
) be the corresponding extension,
with
\eta_{i}
indecomposable,
for every
i\in I
.
Let
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

be the fiber product of

(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}
.
Then there is an isomorphism

\beta\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\eta_{I}\circ\beta=\eta
.

Proof.

Let
f\in H^{2}(G,K)

be the cocycle corresponding to
\eta
.
Then
(K,f)\in\mathcal{H}^{2}
.
Put
(K^{*},S)=X^{2}(K,f)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}
;
then
(K,f)=Y^{2}(K^{*},S)
.

Then the fiber product extension
(
5.7
) corresponds,
by Example
5.9
, to

(\bigoplus_{i\in I}F\varphi_{i},S^{\prime})
,
where

S^{\prime}(\varphi_{i})=f_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
.
The isomorphism

\beta^{*}\colon K^{*}\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}F\varphi_{i}
,
given by
\psi_{i}\mapsto\varphi_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
,
satisfies

S=S^{\prime}\circ\beta^{*}
,
so it induces an isomorphism

(\bigoplus_{i\in I}F\varphi_{i},S^{\prime})\to(K^{*},S)

in
\mathcal{T}^{2}
.
By the duality
there is an isomorphism

(K,f)\to(A^{I},g)
in
\mathcal{H}^{2}
,
where
g
is the cocycle that corresponds to the fiber product.
Thus
there is an isomorphism

\beta\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\eta_{I}\circ\beta=\eta
.
This isomorphism satisfies

\beta^{*}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ\beta
,
for every
\varphi\in(A^{I})^{*}
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

In the above construction of the corresponding extension
(
5.7
)
we have the freedom to choose the basis

\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in I}

of
K^{*}
.
So we may assume that

I=I_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}I_{1}
,
where

\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in I_{0}}
is a basis of
\Ker S

and

\{S(\psi_{i})\}_{i\in I_{1}}
is a basis of
S(K^{*})
.
Then

\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I_{1}}
is a linearly independent subset
of
H^{2}(G,A)

and
(
5.6
) is a copy of the split extension,
for all
i\in I_{0}
.

Another choice of the basis gives the following:

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
\eta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be an epimorphism
such that
K:=\ker\eta

is an
A
-generated module.
Let
L
be a normal subgroup of
H
contained in
K
.
Then there is
a family of indecomposable epimorphisms

\mathcal{H}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G|\,i\in I)
,
with kernel
A
,
and a
G
-isomorphism

\beta\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\eta_{I}\circ\beta=\eta

and

\beta(L)=\{(k_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}\Ker\eta_{i}|\,k_{i}=0\textnormal{ for all }i\notin I^{\prime}\}=\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}\Ker\eta_{i}
,
for some
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.13
,

K=L\oplus M

for some normal subgroup
M
of
H
contained in
K
.
Let

\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in J}

be a basis of
M^{*}
,
and extend every
\psi_{i}
to
\psi_{i}\in K^{*}

by
\psi_{i}|_{L}=0
.
Complete
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in J}
to a basis

\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in I}

of
K^{*}
.
Let
I^{\prime}=I\smallsetminus J
.

Put
(K^{*},S)=X^{2}(K,f)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}
;
then
(K,f)=Y^{2}(K^{*},S)
.

The construction in the proof of
Corollary
5.10
,
gives an isomorphism

\beta\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\eta_{I}\circ\beta=\eta
.
Moreover,
the isomorphism

\beta^{*}\colon K\to K
,
given by

\beta^{*}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ\beta
,
for every
\varphi\in K^{*}
,
maps
\varphi_{i}
onto
\psi_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
.

Let
x\in K
.
Then

x\in L\Leftrightarrow\psi(x)=0\textnormal{ for all }\psi\in M^{*}\Leftrightarrow\psi_{i}(x)=0\textnormal{ for all }i\in J\Leftrightarrow\\
\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{i}(\beta(x))=0\textnormal{ for all }i\in J\Leftrightarrow\beta(x)\in\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}\Ker\eta_{i}.

(5.8)

Thus
\beta(L)=\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}K_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
(K,f)\in\mathcal{H}^{n}(G)
.
Let
\theta\colon{\hat{G}}\twoheadrightarrow G
be an epimorphism
and view the
G
-modules
A
,
K

as
{\hat{G}}
-modules via
\theta
.
Let

\hat{f}=\Inf_{\theta}(f)\in H^{n}({\hat{G}},K)
.
Then the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{K,f}}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{K,\hat{f}}}
\textstyle{H^{n}(G,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Inf_{\theta}}
\textstyle{H^{n}({\hat{G}},A)}

Proof.

Let
f^{\prime}\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K)

be a representative of
f
.
Then
f^{\prime}\circ\theta\in{\bar{Z}}^{n}({\hat{G}},K)

is a representative of
\hat{f}=\Inf_{\theta}(f)
.
As
\Inf_{\theta}
is induced from

\Inf_{\theta}\colon{\bar{Z}}^{n}({\hat{G}},K)\to{\bar{Z}}^{n}(G,K)
,
it suffices to show that

\Inf_{\theta}\circ S_{K,f^{\prime}}=S_{K,f^{\prime}\circ\theta}
.

Let
\varphi\in K^{*}
. Then

S_{K,f^{\prime}}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ f^{\prime}
,
hence

(\Inf_{\theta}\circ S_{K,f^{\prime}})(\varphi)=\Inf_{\theta}(\varphi\circ f^{\prime})=(\varphi\circ f^{\prime})\circ\theta=\varphi\circ(f^{\prime}\circ\theta)=S_{K,f^{\prime}\circ\theta}(\varphi).

∎

6
Multiple fiber products of indecomposable epimorphisms

The preceding section has established some properties of
fiber products
of families of indecomposable epimorphisms,
all of them
with the same abelian kernel.
We now want to extend it to general families
of indecomposable epimorphisms.

We first fix the notation.

Notation 6.1
.

For a profinite group
G

let

(a)

\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
be the set of isomorphism classes of
finite simple
G
-modules;

(b)

\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)

be a set of representatives of the isomorphism classes
of indecomposable epimorphisms onto
G

with non-abelian kernel;

(c)

Put
\Lambda(G)=\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
.

(Although we will not need this,
notice that

\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
is the non-abelian analogue of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Indeed,
every indecomposable epimorphism
\eta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

with a non-abelian kernel
is uniquely determined by its kernel
A

and the outer
G
-action on
A

(
[
Br
, Theorem 6.6]
) –
the non-abelian analogue of a
G
-module.)

Notation 6.2
.

In this section let

\mathcal{H}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G|\,i\in I)

be a family of
indecomposable
epimorphisms
and let

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

be its fiber product.
We keep and enhance
the notation from Section
3
.
So,

(a)

\eta_{I}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G

is the structure map of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and

K_{j}

is the kernel of

\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{j\}}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I\smallsetminus\{j\}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
for every
j\in I
.

Furthermore,

(b)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\mathcal{H})=\{(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})|\,H\textnormal{ is a group and }{\bar{p}}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G\textnormal{ and }\\
p_{i}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H_{i}\textnormal{ satisfy }\eta_{i}\circ p_{i}={\bar{p}},\textnormal{ for every }i\in I\}=\\
\{(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})|\,p_{i}\textnormal{ is an epimorphism, for every }i\in I\}.

(c)

For every
J\subseteq I
denote

K_{J}=\prod_{i\in J}K_{i}
;
in particular,

K_{I}=\Ker\eta_{I}
.

(d)

For every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
let

I_{A}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}A\}

and

F_{A}=\End_{G}(A)
.

(e)

Let

I_{\mathrm{na}}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)\}
.

(f)

For every
\eta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
let

I_{\eta}=\{i\in I|\,\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\eta\}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
L
be a minimal normal subgroup of

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

contained in
K_{I}
.
Then

(a)

if
L
is non-abelian,
then
L=K_{j}
for some
j\in I_{\mathrm{na}}
;

(b)

if
L
is abelian,
then

L\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,
and
L\leq K_{I_{L}}
.

Proof.

Let
j\in I
.
If
K_{j}\neq L
,
then
K_{j}\cap L=1
,
because
K_{j}\cap L
is a proper subgroup of the minimal
K_{j}
.
Then
[K_{j},L]=1
.

(a)
Assume that
L
is non-abelian.
If
K_{j}\neq L
,
then, by the first paragraph of this proof,

1=[\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(K_{j}),\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(L)]=[\Ker\eta_{j},\Ker\eta_{j}]
,
a contradiction to
\Ker\eta_{j}\cong L
being non-abelian.

(b)
Assume that
L
is abelian.
Then, by the first paragraph of this proof,

[K_{i},L]=1
,
that is,
K_{i}\leq C_{K_{I}}(L)
,
for every
i\in I
.
So

K_{I}\leq C_{K_{I}}(L)
,
that is,

L\leq Z(K_{I})
.
By Lemma
1.6
(a),

L
is a
G
-module, that is,
L\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Let
j\in I\smallsetminus I_{L}
.
Then

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(L)=1
.
Indeed, assume the contrary.
As
L\leq K_{I}
and

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(K_{I})=\Ker\eta_{j}
,
we have

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(L)\leq\Ker\eta_{j}
.
So, by Lemma
1.4
(a),

\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}(L)=\Ker\eta_{j}

and

L\cong\Ker\eta_{j}

via
\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}
.
By Lemma
1.6
(d)
this is an isomorphism of
G
-modules,
and hence
j\in I_{L}
,
a contradiction.

Thus

L\leq\prod_{j\in I_{L}}K_{j}=K_{I_{L}}
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and let
L
be a normal subgroup of
H
contained in
K_{I}=\Ker\eta_{I}
.
Then

K_{I}=(\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}}K_{i})\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}K_{I_{A}}

and

L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}}(L\cap K_{i})\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big(L\cap K_{I_{A}}\big)=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na},L}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big(L\cap K_{I_{A}}\big),

(6.1)

where

I_{\mathrm{na},L}=\{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}|\,K_{i}\leq L\}
.

Proof.

The presentation of
K_{I}

follows from

I=I_{\mathrm{na}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}I_{A}
.
If
i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}
, then

L\cap K_{i}=\begin{cases}K_{i}&\textnormal{ if $K_{i}\leq L$}\\
1&\textnormal{ if $K_{i}\not\leq L$}\end{cases}
,
because
K_{i}
is a minimal normal subgroup in
H
.
Thus the two presentations of
L
in (
6.1
) are equivalent.
We note that the above decomposition of
I
also implies,
by Remark
3.2
(d),
that

H=(\underset{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i})\times_{G}\underset{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}(\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}).

(6.2)

First assume that
I
is finite.
Then
K_{I}
and
L
are finite.
The case
L=1
being trivial,
we assume
|L|>1
and proceed by induction on
|L|
.
There is a minimal normal subgroup
N
of
H

contained in
L
.
By Lemma
6.3
:
If
N
is non-abelian,
there is
j\in I_{\mathrm{na}}
such that

N=K_{j}
.
If
N
is abelian,
there is a unique
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
such that
N\cong_{G}A
,
and

N\leq K_{I_{A}}
;
by Corollary
5.12

we may assume that

N=K_{j}
for some
j\in I_{A}
.
Thus in both cases

N
is contained in the right handed side of
(
6.1
).

Put
J=I\smallsetminus\{j\}

and
{\bar{H}}=\underset{i\in J\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Then
N
is the kernel of

\mathrm{pr}_{I,J}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{H}}
.
By the induction hypothesis, the image
{\bar{L}}
of
L
in
{\bar{H}}

is of the form

{\bar{L}}=\prod_{i\in J_{\mathrm{na},{\bar{L}}}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big({\bar{L}}\cap\prod_{i\in I_{A}\smallsetminus\{j\}}K_{i}\big),

where

J_{\mathrm{na},{\bar{L}}}=\{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}\smallsetminus\{j\}|\,K_{i}\leq{\bar{L}}\}=I_{\mathrm{na},L}\smallsetminus\{j\}
.
The right handed side of (
6.1
)
maps onto
{\bar{L}}

and contains
N
.
Hence by the third isomorphism theorem
that group is
L
.

Now assume that
I
is infinite.
Then

H=\varprojlim_{I^{\prime}}\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and

K_{I}=\varprojlim_{I^{\prime}}\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}K_{i}
,
where
I^{\prime}
runs through the collection of finite subsets of
I
.
The kernel of

\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is
K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}
.
By the finite case,
the image

LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}

of
L
in
\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is of the form

\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}_{\mathrm{na}}}(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})\cap(K_{i}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})\quad\times\quad\\
\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})\cap(K_{I^{\prime}_{A}}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})=\\
=\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}_{\mathrm{na}}}(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\cap K_{i}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\quad\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\cap K_{I^{\prime}_{A}}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}.

If
I^{\prime}\supseteq I^{\prime\prime}
,
then the map

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I^{\prime\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

of the inverse system
maps
the factors into the corresponding factors,
that is,

\bullet

(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\cap K_{i}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}

into

(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime\prime}}\cap K_{i}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime\prime}})/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime\prime}}
,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}_{\mathrm{na}}
;
and

\bullet

(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\cap K_{A}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}

into

(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime\prime}}\cap K_{A}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime\prime}})/K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime\prime}}
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Therefore

L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}}M_{i}\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}M_{A}
,
where,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}_{\mathrm{na}}
,

M_{i}=\bigcap_{I^{\prime}}(LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\cap K_{i}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}})=\big(\bigcap_{I^{\prime}}LK_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\big)\cap\big(\bigcap_{I^{\prime}}K_{i}K_{I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}\big)=L\cap K_{i}.

Similarly,

M_{A}=L\cap K_{A}

for every simple
G
-module
A
.
This gives the desired presentation of
L
.
∎

We can simplify the presentation of
L

if we present

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

as a fiber product of a different family
of indecomposable epimorphisms
(which depends on
L
):

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and let
L
be a normal subgroup of
H
contained in
\Ker\eta_{I}
.
Then there is a family

\bar{\mathcal{H}}=({\bar{\eta}}_{i}\colon{\bar{H}}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

of indecomposable epimorphisms
and an isomorphism

\omega\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}

such that

{\bar{\eta}}_{I}\circ\omega=\eta_{I}

and
\omega(L)=\prod_{i\in{\bar{I}}}{\bar{K}}_{i}
,
for some
{\bar{I}}\subseteq I
.

Here

{\bar{\eta}}_{I}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G

is the fiber product of
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
,
with projection maps

{\overline{\mathrm{pr}}}_{I,{\bar{I}}}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in{\bar{I}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}
,
for
{\bar{I}}\subseteq I
,
and

{\bar{K}}_{i}=\Ker{\overline{\mathrm{pr}}}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{i\}}
,
for every
i\in I
.

Proof.

If
I=I_{A}
for some
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,
the assertion follows by
Corollary
5.12
.

In the general case

I=I_{\mathrm{na}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}I_{A}
,
so, by Remark
3.2
(d),

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=(\underset{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i})\times_{G}\underset{A\in\lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}(\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}).

By Proposition
6.4
,

L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na},L}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big(L\cap K_{I_{A}}\big).

By the first paragraph,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

there is an isomorphism

\omega_{A}\colon\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}

that maps
L\cap K_{I_{A}}
onto
\prod_{i\in{\bar{I}}_{A}}{\bar{K}}_{i}
,
for some
{\bar{I}}_{A}\subseteq I_{A}
.
Then
the identities of
H_{i}
,
for
i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}
,
together with the isomorphisms

\omega_{A}
,
for
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,
define an isomorphism

\omega

that maps
L
onto

\prod_{i\in{\bar{I}}}{\bar{K}}_{i}
,
where

{\bar{I}}=I_{\mathrm{na},L}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}{\bar{I}}_{A}\subseteq I
.
∎

Theorem
6.7

below is the main technical result of this paper:
It characterizes the indecomposable covers of
a profinite group
G

dominated by a fiber product of indecomposable covers of
G
.
We deduce it from an even more general characterization –
of indecomposable covers of a quotient of
G
.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let

\pi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow D
,
and let

\zeta\colon E\twoheadrightarrow D
be indecomposable.
Put
C=\Ker\zeta
;
if
C
is non-abelian,
let

\hat{\zeta}=\Inf_{\pi}\zeta

and

I_{\hat{\zeta}}=\{i\in I|\,\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\hat{\zeta}\}
,
and
if
C
is abelian,
let

{\hat{C}}=\Inf_{\pi}C

and

I_{{\hat{C}}}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}{\hat{C}}\}
.
Then

(*)

there is

\varepsilon\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that
the following square is semi-cartesian

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{D}

(6.3)

if and only if
exactly one of the following two conditions holds:

(a)

C
is non-abelian
and

I_{\hat{\zeta}}\neq\emptyset
.

(b)

C
is abelian,

I_{{\hat{C}}}\neq\emptyset
,
and
\hat{\zeta}
is
a nontrivial linear combination
of
(\eta_{i})_{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}}

over
F_{{\hat{C}}}=\End_{G}({\hat{C}})
(in the sense of Remark
5.4
(b)).

Moreover,
if (
6.3
) is semi-cartesian,
then

(a’)

if
C
is non-abelian,
there is a unique
j\in I

such that

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\Ker\varepsilon\cap\Ker\eta_{I}
;
this
j
is in
I_{\hat{\zeta}}
and
there is
\varepsilon_{j}\colon H_{j}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that
(
6.4
) below commutes
and
the square in it is cartesian.

(b’)

if
C
is abelian,
there is

\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon\underset{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that

\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{{\hat{C}}}}
.

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{H_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{j}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{D}

(6.4)

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{\hat{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I_{\hat{C}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{\varepsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I_{\hat{C}}}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\zeta}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{D}

(6.5)

Proof.

Let
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
First assume (*).
Let
K=\Ker\eta_{I}
and
N=\Ker\varepsilon
.
As (
6.3
) is semi-cartesian,

\Ker\pi\circ\eta_{I}=\Ker\zeta\circ\varepsilon=KN
.
Put
L=K\cap N
.
By Corollary
2.6
,

K\not\leq N
,
hence
L\subsetneqq K
.
We may assume that

E=H/N
and
\varepsilon
is the quotient map

H\twoheadrightarrow H/N
,
and
\zeta
is the quotient map

H/N\twoheadrightarrow H/KN
.
Hence

C=\Ker\zeta=K/L
.
By Proposition
6.4

(using its notation

\{K_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
I_{\mathrm{na}}
,
I_{\mathrm{na},L}
,
I_{A}
)

K=\big(\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}}K_{i}\big)\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big(\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}\big)\ \textnormal{ and }\ L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na},L}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big(L\cap\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}\big),

(6.6)

and hence,
by the second isomorphism theorem for groups,

C=K/L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}\smallsetminus I_{\mathrm{na},L}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}\big(\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}\big)L/L.

(6.7)

The right handed side is a direct product
of normal subgroups in
E
.
However, as
C
is a minimal normal subgroup in
E
,
exactly one direct factor in this presentation
is non-trivial.
So, there are two cases:

(i)

C
is non-abelian.
Then the nontrivial factor in (
6.7
) is
K_{j}

for a unique
j\in I_{\mathrm{na}}
.
Hence

I_{\mathrm{na}}\smallsetminus I_{\mathrm{na},L}=\{j\}

and

\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}\leq L

for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Then

K_{j}
is non-abelian and (
6.6
) gives

K\cap N=L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}\smallsetminus\{j\}}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A}\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}=\prod_{i\in I\smallsetminus\{j\}}K_{i}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}.

Thus
there is
\varepsilon_{j}\colon H_{j}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that

\varepsilon_{j}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\varepsilon
.
From the commutativity of (
6.3
) we deduce that
(
6.4
) commutes.
By Lemma
2.8
(a)
the square in (
6.4
)
is semi-cartesian.
As

\Ker\varepsilon\cap\Ker\eta_{I}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}
,
we have

\Ker\varepsilon_{j}\cap\Ker\eta_{j}=1
,
hence the square in (
6.4
) is cartesian.
Thus

\hat{\zeta}=\Inf_{\pi}\zeta\cong_{G}\eta_{j}
,
and hence

j\in I_{\hat{\zeta}}\neq\emptyset
.
Finally,
j\in I
with

L=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}

is unique,
because
distinct
j
’s in
I
give distinct

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}
’s.
This gives (a) and (a’).

(ii)

C
is abelian.
Then

I_{\mathrm{na}}\smallsetminus I_{\mathrm{na},L}=\emptyset
,
and

\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}\leq L

for all but a unique
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
This
A
satisfies

\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i}\not\leq N
,
that is,

\varepsilon(\prod_{i\in I_{A}}K_{i})\neq 1
,
so there is
j\in I_{A}

(hence
A\cong_{G}K_{j}
)
such that

\varepsilon(K_{j})\neq 1
.
As
K_{j}\leq K=\Ker\eta_{I}
,
we have
\varepsilon(K_{j})\leq\varepsilon(K)=\Ker\zeta=C
.
By Lemma
1.4
(a),
the restriction
\varepsilon|_{K_{j}}\colon K_{j}\to C

is an isomorphism.
In particular,

K_{j}\cong_{G}{\hat{C}}
,
by Lemma
1.6
(e),
and hence

A\cong_{G}{\hat{C}}
.
So
j\in I_{{\hat{C}}}
,
whence
I_{{\hat{C}}}\neq\emptyset
,
and

L=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}}K_{i}\times\prod_{A^{\prime}\neq{\hat{C}}}\prod_{i\in I_{A^{\prime}}}K_{i}\ \times\big(L\cap\prod_{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}}K_{i}\big)\supseteq\kern-3.0pt\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{na}}}K_{i}\times\kern-2.0pt\prod_{A^{\prime}\neq{\hat{C}}}\prod_{i\in I_{A^{\prime}}}K_{i}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{{\hat{C}}}}.

So
\varepsilon\colon H\twoheadrightarrow E

induces

{\bar{\varepsilon}}\colon\underset{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that

{\bar{\varepsilon}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{{\hat{C}}}}=\varepsilon

in (
6.5
).
It follows that

\zeta\circ{\bar{\varepsilon}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{{\hat{C}}}}=\zeta\circ\varepsilon=\pi\circ\eta_{I}=\pi\circ\eta_{I_{{\hat{C}}}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{{\hat{C}}}}
,
and hence

\zeta\circ{\bar{\varepsilon}}=\pi\circ\eta_{I_{{\hat{C}}}}
.
By Lemma
2.8
(a)
the square formed by
{\bar{\varepsilon}},\zeta,\eta_{I_{{\hat{C}}}},\pi

is still semi-cartesian.

Let
H^{\prime}=G\times_{D}E

and let
\rho\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow E
be the projection
of the fiber product.
The other projection

H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G
is, by definition,
\hat{\zeta}=\Inf_{\pi}(\zeta)
.
The kernel of
\hat{\zeta}
is
{\hat{C}}

and
\rho
maps
{\hat{C}}
isomorphically onto
C
.
There is a unique homomorphism
\varepsilon^{\prime}
such that
(
6.5
)
commutes.
By Lemma
2.8
(b),

\varepsilon^{\prime}
is an epimorphism.

The restriction of
\varepsilon^{\prime}
to

K_{I_{\hat{C}}}=\Ker\eta_{I_{\hat{C}}}

is a homomorphism
\beta
of
G
-modules
such that

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{I_{\hat{C}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I_{\hat{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\hat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\zeta}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

(6.8)

commutes.
Clearly,
\beta
is surjective.
By Lemma
5.1
,

\beta_{*}(\eta_{I_{\hat{C}}})=\hat{\zeta}
.

As
\beta
is a
G
-homomorphism,

\alpha_{i}:=\beta|_{K_{i}}
is a
G
-homomorphism,
for all
i\in I_{\hat{C}}
.
As
\beta
is continuous,

\alpha_{i}=0

for all but finitely many
i\in I_{\hat{C}}
.
So
\beta\colon K_{I_{\hat{C}}}=\prod_{i\in I_{\hat{C}}}K_{i}\twoheadrightarrow{\hat{C}}

is of the form

(a_{i})_{i\in I_{\hat{C}}}\mapsto\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(a_{i})
.
As
\beta
is surjective,
not every
\alpha_{i}
is zero.

For every
i\in I_{\hat{C}}

choose
f_{i}\in{\bar{Z}}^{2}(G,{\hat{C}})
representing
\eta_{i}
.
By Lemma
5.5
,

\eta_{I_{\hat{C}}}\colon H\to G
is represented by a cochain

g\in{\bar{Z}}^{2}(G,K_{I_{\hat{C}}})

given by

g(\sigma,\tau)=\big(f_{i}(\sigma,\tau)\big)_{i\in I_{\hat{C}}}
.
Then,
for all
\sigma,\tau\in G
,

\big(\beta_{*}(g)\big)(\sigma,\tau)=\beta\big(g(\sigma,\tau)\big)=\beta\big(f_{i}(\sigma,\tau)_{i\in I_{\hat{C}}}\big)=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}f_{i}(\sigma,\tau),

whence

\beta_{*}(g)=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}f_{i}
.
Hence

\hat{\zeta}=\beta_{*}(\eta_{I{\hat{C}}})=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\eta_{i}
.

This finishes the proof of (b) and (b’).

Conversely,
suppose that (a) or (b) holds.
We have to show (*).

If (a) holds,
fix
j\in I_{\hat{\zeta}}
,
so that
\eta_{j}\cong_{G}\hat{\zeta}
.
By the definition of
\hat{\zeta}

there is

\varepsilon_{j}\colon H_{j}\twoheadrightarrow E

be such that
the square in (
6.4
) is cartesian.
Then

\varepsilon:=\varepsilon_{j}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}

makes (
6.4
) commutative,
and hence (
6.3
) commutes.
By Lemma
2.8
(a) it is
a semi-cartesian square.

If (b) holds,
the definition of
\hat{\zeta}

gives a cartesian square
like the one in (
6.5
),
and

\hat{\zeta}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\eta_{i}

for some

\{0\}\neq\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}}\subseteq F_{{\hat{C}}}
.
The map

\beta\colon\prod_{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}}K_{i}\to{\hat{C}}

given by

(a_{i})_{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}}\mapsto\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(a_{i})

is a homomorphism of
G
-modules.
It is non-trivial and
{\hat{C}}
is simple,
hence
\beta
is surjective.
By Lemma
5.1

it extends to

\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon\underset{i\in I_{{\hat{C}}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}

such that (
6.8
) commutes.
Let

{\bar{\varepsilon}}=\rho\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}

and

\varepsilon={\bar{\varepsilon}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{{\hat{C}}}}
.
Then
(
6.5
) commutes.
By Lemma
2.8
(a),
(
6.3
) is a semi-cartesian square.
∎

We write out
the particular case of
\pi=\mathrm{id}_{G}
:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let

\zeta\colon E\twoheadrightarrow G
be indecomposable.
Let
C=\Ker\zeta
;
if
C
is non-abelian,
let

I_{\zeta}=\{i\in I|\,\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\zeta\}
,
and
if
C
is abelian,
let

I_{C}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}C\}
.
Then there is

\varepsilon\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that

\zeta\circ\varepsilon=\eta_{I}

if and only if
exactly one of the following two conditions holds:

(a)

C
is non-abelian
and

I_{\zeta}\neq\emptyset
.

(b)

C
is abelian,

I_{C}\neq\emptyset
,
and
\zeta
is
a nontrivial linear combination
of
(\eta_{i})_{i\in I_{C}}

over
F_{C}=\End_{G}(C)
(in the sense of Remark
5.4
(b)).

Moreover,
if
\varepsilon
is an epimorphism
such that

\zeta\circ\varepsilon=\eta_{I}
,
then

(a’)

if
C
is non-abelian,
there is a unique
j\in I

such that
\Ker\varepsilon=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}
;
this
j
is in
I_{\zeta}
and
there is an isomorphism
\varepsilon_{j}\colon H_{j}\to E

such that

\varepsilon_{j}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\varepsilon

and

\zeta\circ\varepsilon_{j}=\eta_{j}
.

(b’)

if
C
is abelian,
there is

\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon\underset{i\in I_{C}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow E

such that

\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{C}}

and hence

\zeta\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}=\eta_{I_{C}}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)

let
H_{\lambda}=\underset{i\in I_{\lambda}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Then

\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}
,
the fiber product of

(\eta_{I_{\lambda}}\colon H_{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)}
,
is compact.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.8

we have to show
for every
finite
\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda(G)
,

\mu\in\Lambda(G)\smallsetminus\Lambda
,
and

{\hat{\Lambda}}=\Lambda\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\mu\}

that
the square in

\textstyle{\underset{\lambda\in{\hat{\Lambda}}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{{\hat{\Lambda}},\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{{\hat{\Lambda}},\mu}}
\textstyle{\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I_{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{H_{\mu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I_{\mu}}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{G}

(6.9)

is compact.
Thus,
by Definition
2.9
(d),
we have to show that
there are no epimorphisms
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime},\zeta

such that

\zeta
is indecomposable,

\zeta\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}=\eta_{I_{\Lambda}}

and

\zeta\circ\varepsilon=\eta_{I_{\mu}}
.

Assume that there is such a diagram.
Then
\zeta
is dominated both by
\eta_{I_{\Lambda}}
and
\eta_{I_{\mu}}
.
By definition

H_{\mu}
is the fiber product of

(\eta_{i})_{i\in I_{\mu}}
;
by Remark
3.2
(d),

\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}

is the fiber product of

(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I_{\lambda}}
.
If
C=\Ker\zeta
is non-abelian,
then by Theorem
6.7
,
there is a unique
j\in I

such that

\eta_{j}\cong_{G}\zeta

and

j\in I_{\mu}
and

j\in\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I_{\lambda}
,
so
\mu\in\Lambda
,
a contradiction.
If
C
is abelian,
then by Theorem
6.7
,

\mu=C
and
C\in\Lambda
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Let

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\mathcal{H})
.
If
I=I_{\mathrm{na}}

and

\Ker p_{i}\neq\Ker p_{j}

for distinct
i,j\in I
,
then the induced map
p_{I}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is surjective.

Proof.

We have to show condition (b) of Lemma
3.4
.
So let
I^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{j\}\subseteq I

and assume that
p_{I^{\prime}}
in (
3.3
) is surjective.
If (
3.3
) is not semi-cartesian,
by Corollary
2.6

there is
\varepsilon\colon\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow H_{j}

such that

\varepsilon\circ p_{I^{\prime}}=p_{j}
,
and hence

\eta_{j}\circ\varepsilon=\eta_{I^{\prime}}
.
By Theorem
6.7

there is a unique
i\in I^{\prime}

such that

\Ker\varepsilon=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}
.
Thus

p_{I^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Ker\varepsilon)=p_{I^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i})
.
As

\varepsilon\circ p_{I^{\prime}}=p_{j}

and

\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ p_{I^{\prime}}=p_{i}
,
the above equation is

\Ker p_{j}=\Ker p_{i}
,
a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

Let

\mathcal{H}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

be a family of indecomposable epimorphisms
and let

(H,\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I},{\bar{p}})\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\mathcal{H})
.
Assume that

\Ker p_{i}\neq\Ker p_{j}

for distinct
i,j\in I_{\mathrm{na}}
,
and that the induced map

p_{A}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is surjective,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Then the induced map
p_{I}\colon H\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is surjective.
If also

\bigcap_{i\in I}\Ker p_{i}=1
,
then
p_{I}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

As

I=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)}I_{\lambda}
,
by Remark
3.2
(d)
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}
,
where

H_{\lambda}=\underset{i\in I_{\lambda}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is the fiber product of

(\eta_{I_{\lambda}}\colon H_{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)}
,
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)
.

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)

let

p_{\lambda}\colon H\to H_{\lambda}

be the map induced from
(p_{i})_{i\in I_{\lambda}}
.
Then
p_{I}
is the map

H\to\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}

induced from
(p_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)}
.
If
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
,
then
p_{\lambda}
is surjective
by Lemma
6.9
.
If
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,
then
p_{\lambda}
is surjective by assumption.
By Lemma
6.8
,

\underset{i\in I_{\lambda}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is compact.
Hence
p
is surjective.

The last assertion follows from
Lemma
3.3
(a).
∎

Corollary 6.11
.

Assume that
(\eta_{i})_{i\in I}

are pairwise non-isomorphic
indecomposable epimorphisms,

I\neq\emptyset
,
and for every abelian simple
G
-module
A

the family

T_{A}=(\eta_{i}|\,i\in I,\ \Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}A,\ \eta_{i}\textnormal{ does not split})

is linearly independent over
\End_{G}(A)
.
Then the fiber product

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is compact.

Proof.

Let
I^{\prime}
be a finite subset of
I

and
j\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}
.
Let

\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

and

\pi\colon G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G

be epimorphisms,

\pi
indecomposable,
such that

\pi\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}=\eta_{I^{\prime}}
.
By Lemma
3.8

it suffices to show that
there is no

\varepsilon_{j}\colon H_{j}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

such that

\pi\circ\varepsilon_{j}=\eta_{j}
,
that is,
since
\eta_{j}
is indecomposable,
that

\eta_{j}\not\cong_{G}\pi
.

Denote

A=\Ker\pi
.
By Theorem
6.6

either

(a)

A
is non-abelian and there is
k\in I^{\prime}
such that

\eta_{k}\cong_{G}\pi
;
or

(b)

A
is abelian and

\pi
is a nontrivial linear combination of

T^{\prime}_{A}=\{\eta_{i}|\,i\in I^{\prime},\ \Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}A\}
.

In the first case

j\neq k
,
hence

\eta_{j}\not\cong_{G}\eta_{k}
,
whence

\eta_{j}\not\cong_{G}\pi
.

In the second case,
if
\eta_{j}
is the split extension
(corresponding to
0\in H^{2}(G,A)
),
then no
\eta_{i}\in T^{\prime}_{A}
can be split,
because
i\in I^{\prime}
, and hence
i\neq j
.
Thus
T^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq T_{A}
,
hence a nontrivial linear combination of
elements of
T^{\prime}_{A}

is not
0
,
whence not isomorphic to
\eta_{j}
.

If, in the second case,

\eta_{j}
is not split,
then
\eta_{j}\in T_{A}
, hence
\eta_{j}

is linearly independent
of
T^{\prime}_{A}
,
whence
\pi
is not isomorphic to
\eta_{j}
.
∎

7
Epimorphisms between fiber products
of indecomposable epimorphisms

Fix a profinite group
G

and recall the notation

\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,

\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
,
and

\Lambda(G)

from Notation
6.1
.

Let

\mathcal{H}=(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

be a family
of indecomposable epimorphisms.
We will consider the map

\eta_{I}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
.
For every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

denote

I_{A}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}A\}

and

H_{A}=\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Let

B_{A}=\Ker(\eta_{I_{A}}\colon H_{A}\twoheadrightarrow G)
,
let

f_{A}\in H^{2}(G,B_{A})

be the cocycle corresponding to the extension

1\to B_{A}\to H_{A}\to G\to 1
,
and let
(V_{A},S_{A})=X^{2}(B_{A},f_{A})
,
where
V_{A}
is a vector space over
F_{A}=\End_{G}(A)
.
Then

\Img S_{A}
is an
F_{A}
-subspace of

H^{2}(G,A)

and

\dim\Ker S_{A}

is a cardinality.

For every indecomposable cover
\zeta
of
G

denote

I_{\zeta}=\{i\in I|\,\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\zeta\}
.

Notice that

I=\big(\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)}I_{\zeta}\big)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\big(\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}I_{A}\big)
.

Now let

\pi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

be an epimorphism,
and let

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=(\eta^{\prime}_{i}\colon H^{\prime}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime})_{i\in I^{\prime}}

be a family
of indecomposable covers of
G^{\prime}
.
For
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda(G^{\prime})

and for an indecomposable cover
\zeta^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}

we define
in the same way
the invariants

\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\colon\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
,

I^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,

H^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,

B_{A^{\prime}}
,

f^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,

V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,

S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,

F^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,
and

I^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}
.

We address the question,
when there is

\theta\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}

such that

\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\pi\circ\eta_{I}
.
But first we assume that

G^{\prime}=G
and
\pi=\mathrm{id}_{G}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Assume that

G^{\prime}=G
and
\pi=\mathrm{id}_{G}
.
There is
\theta\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}

such that

\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\eta_{I}

if and only if

(a)

|I^{\prime}_{\zeta}|\leq|I_{\zeta}|
,
for every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
;
and

(b)

\Img S^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq\Img S_{A}

and

\dim\Ker S^{\prime}_{A}\leq\dim\Ker S_{A}
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Proof.

Put
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,

H^{\prime}=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}
,

K=\Ker\eta_{I}
,
and

K^{\prime}=\Ker\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
.

Part A:

If
\theta
exists, then (a) holds.

Fix
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
.
Let
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Then

\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ(\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ\theta)=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\eta_{I}
.
By Theorem
6.7
(a’)
there is a unique
j\in I_{\zeta}

such that

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=\Ker(\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ\theta)=\theta^{-1}(\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i})
.
This defines a map

i\mapsto j

from
I^{\prime}_{\zeta}
to
I_{\zeta}
.
This map is injective:
If
i_{1},i_{2}\in I^{\prime}_{\zeta}
map onto the same
j\in I_{\zeta}
,
then

\theta^{-1}(\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i_{1}})=\theta^{-1}(\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i_{2}})
,
hence

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i_{1}}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i_{2}}
,
whence

i_{1}=i_{2}
,
by
Theorem
6.7
(a’).
Thus

|I^{\prime}_{\zeta}|\leq|I_{\zeta}|
.

Part B:

If
\theta
exists, then (b) holds.

Fix
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

and let

F_{A}=\End_{G}(A)
.

For every
i\in I^{\prime}_{A}
,
\varepsilon_{i}:=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ\theta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}_{i}

satisfies

\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\varepsilon_{i}=\eta_{I}
.
By Theorem
6.7
(b’),
there is an epimorphism
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\colon H_{A}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}_{i}

such that

\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}=\varepsilon_{i}=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ\theta
.
We have

\eta_{I_{A}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}=\eta_{I}=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ\theta=\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}},

hence

\eta_{I_{A}}=\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}
.
Thus

\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}

does not depend on
i
.
By the universal property of the fiber product

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}
,
(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}_{A}}

defines a homomorphism

\theta_{A}\colon H_{A}\to H^{\prime}_{A}

such that

\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime}_{A},i}\circ\theta_{A}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}

for all
i\in I^{\prime}_{A}
.
So

\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\theta_{A}=\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime}_{A},i}\circ\theta_{A}=\eta^{\prime}_{i}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{I_{A}}.

We claim that

\theta_{A}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\theta.

(7.1)

Indeed,
both sides are maps

H\to H^{\prime}_{A}=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}
,
and for every
i\in I^{\prime}_{A}

\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime}_{A},i}\circ\theta_{A}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}=\varepsilon_{i}=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}\circ\theta=\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime}_{A},i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\theta,

hence
by the universal property of the fiber product
both sides of (
7.1
) are equal.
In particular, as

\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{A}},\theta
are surjective, so is
\theta_{A}
.
We have

\eta_{I_{A}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}=\eta_{I}=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\theta=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\theta_{A}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}},

hence

\eta_{I_{A}}=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}_{A}}\circ\theta_{A}.

(7.2)

\textstyle{H_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{H_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I_{A}}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime}_{A},i}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}_{A}}}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{A}}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G}

Thus,
replacing
\theta
by
\theta_{A}
,
we may assume that
I=I_{A}
and
I^{\prime}=I^{\prime}_{A}
.

By the equivalence of categories
\mathcal{T}^{2}
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}

of Proposition
5.8
,

\theta
induces an
F_{A}
-linear map

T_{A}\colon V^{\prime}_{A}\to V_{A}

such that

S_{A}\circ T_{A}=S^{\prime}_{A}
.
As
\theta
is surjective,

T_{A}
is injective
(Remark
4.12
).
The last equation implies that

\Img S^{\prime}_{A}=S_{A}(\Img T_{A})\subseteq\Img S_{A}

and

T_{A}(\Ker S^{\prime}_{A})\subseteq\Ker S_{A}
.
This gives (b).

Part C:

If (a) and (b) hold, then
\theta
exists.

Let
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
.
By (a) we may assume that

I^{\prime}_{\zeta}\subseteq I_{\zeta}
.
As
\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\eta^{\prime}_{i}\cong_{G}\zeta
,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}_{\zeta}
,
we may assume that

H^{\prime}_{i}=H_{i}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{i}
,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}_{\zeta}
.

Let
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
By (b)
there is a basis
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in J_{A}}
of
\Img S_{A}

such that,
for some
J^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq J_{A}
,
the set

\{f_{i}\}_{i\in J^{\prime}_{A}}
is a basis of
\Img S^{\prime}_{A}
.
By Remark
5.11
we may assume that

I_{A}=J_{A}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}R_{A}
,
where

|R_{A}|=\dim\Ker S_{A}

and the extension
\eta_{i}
of
G
by
A

corresponds to

\begin{cases}f_{i}&\textnormal{if $i\in J_{A}$}\\
0&\textnormal{if $i\in R_{A}$}\end{cases}\in H^{2}(G,A)
.
Similarly we may assume that

I^{\prime}_{A}=J^{\prime}_{A}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}R^{\prime}_{A}
,
where

|R^{\prime}_{A}|=\dim\Ker S^{\prime}_{A}

and the extension
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
of
G
by
A

corresponds to

\begin{cases}f_{i}&\textnormal{if $i\in J^{\prime}_{A}$}\\
0&\textnormal{if $i\in R^{\prime}_{A}$}\end{cases}\in H^{2}(G,A)
.
As
|R^{\prime}_{A}|\leq|R_{A}|
,
without loss of generality
R^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq R_{A}
.
Thus
I^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq I_{A}

and
H^{\prime}_{i}=H_{i}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{i}
,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}_{A}
.

After these adjustments,
it follows that
I^{\prime}\subseteq I

and
H^{\prime}_{i}=H_{i}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{i}
,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\theta:=\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}

satisfies

\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\eta_{I}
.
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

Assume that

G^{\prime}=G
and
\pi=\mathrm{id}_{G}
.
There is an isomorphism

\theta\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}

such that
\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\theta=\eta_{I}

if and only if

(a)

|I_{\zeta}|=|I^{\prime}_{\zeta}|
,
for every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
;
and

(b)

\Img S_{A}=\Img S^{\prime}_{A}

and

\dim\Ker S_{A}=\dim\Ker S^{\prime}_{A}
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Proof.

Conditions (a), (b) are necessary by
Theorem
7.1
.
If they hold,
in Part C of the proof of Theorem
7.1

we may assume that

I^{\prime}=I

and
H^{\prime}_{i}=H_{i}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
.
Therefore

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Assume that

G^{\prime}=G
and
\pi=\mathrm{id}_{G}
.
Suppose there are epimorphisms

\rho\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}

and

\rho^{\prime}\colon\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

such that

\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\rho=\eta_{I}

and

\eta_{I}\circ\rho^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
.
Then there is an isomorphism

\theta\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}

such that
\eta_{I}\circ\theta=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.1
,
the existence of
\rho
implies
conditions (a), (b) of Theorem
7.1
.
Similarly,
the existence of
\rho^{\prime}
implies
the symmetrical conditions.
Thus conditions (a), (b) of Corollary
7.2

hold.
The conclusion now follows by Corollary
7.2
.
∎

Now we approach the general case,
of an epimorphism
\pi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
.

Every
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})

can be also viewed as a
G
-module
(with
G
acting on
A^{\prime}
via
\pi
),
denoted
\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})
,
in
\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Let

\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}
denote the inflation

H^{2}(G^{\prime},A^{\prime})\to H^{2}(G,\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime}))
.
Every indecomposable cover

\zeta^{\prime}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

of
G^{\prime}

defines an indecomposable cover

\zeta:=\Inf_{\pi}(\zeta^{\prime})\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

of
G
.
If
\Ker\zeta^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})
,
then

\Ker\zeta=\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Theorem 7.4
.

There is
\theta

such that the following square is semi-cartesian

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.3)

if and only if

(a)

|I^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}|\leq|I_{\zeta}|
,
for every
\zeta^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G^{\prime})

and
\zeta:=\Inf_{\pi}(\zeta^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
;
and

(b)

\Img(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})\subseteq\Img S_{A}

and

\dim\Ker(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})\leq\dim\Ker S_{A}
,
for every
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})

and
A:=\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Proof.

Put
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,

H^{\prime}=\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}H^{\prime}_{i}
,

K=\Ker\eta_{I}
,
and

K^{\prime}=\Ker\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
.

Part A:

A special case.

Let
I=I^{\prime}

and,
for every
i\in I
,
let

H_{i}=G\times_{G^{\prime}}H^{\prime}_{i}

(with respect to
\pi,\eta^{\prime}_{i}
)
and
\eta_{i}=\Inf_{\pi}(\eta^{\prime}_{i})\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}

be the projection on the first coordinate.
By Remark
3.2
(h),

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

can be viewed as

G\times_{G^{\prime}}(\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i})

with respect to
\pi
and
\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
,
so there is a cartesian square (
7.3
).

For every
\zeta^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G^{\prime})
and
\zeta=\Inf_{\pi}(\zeta^{\prime})

we have

I_{\zeta}=I^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}
,
hence (a) holds.

Let
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})
.
If we replace
I=I^{\prime}
by
I_{A^{\prime}}=I^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
,
then,
by Remark
3.2
(h),
we still get a cartesian square (
7.3
),
say, with
\theta_{A}
instead of
\theta
.
Then
\theta_{A}
maps
K
isomorphically onto
K^{\prime}
.
So there is an isomorphism

\theta_{A}^{*}\colon V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}=(K^{\prime})^{*}\to V_{A}=K^{*}

and it follows from the definitions that
there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{V_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{A}}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{A}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(G,A)}
\textstyle{H^{2}(G^{\prime},A^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}}

(7.4)

Thus

\Img(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=\Img S_{A}

and

\dim\Ker(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=\dim\Ker S_{A}
,
and hence (b) holds.

Part B:

The general case.

By Part A
there is
a fiber product

\eta^{\prime\prime}_{I^{\prime\prime}}\colon H^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G

of indecomposables
(\eta^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime\prime}}

with
I^{\prime\prime}=I^{\prime}
and
the invariant

S^{\prime\prime}_{A}\colon V^{\prime\prime}_{A}\to H^{2}(G,A)
,
for every
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})

and
A:=\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,
and there is
a cartesian square
- the right-handed square in (
7.5
) below -

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime\prime}_{I^{\prime\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.5)

such that

(a’)

|I^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}|=|I^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta}|
,
for every
\zeta^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G^{\prime})

and
\zeta:=\Inf_{\pi}(\zeta^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
;
and

(b’)

\Img(\Inf_{\pi,A}\circ S^{\prime}_{A})=\Img S^{\prime\prime}_{A}

and

\dim\Ker(\Inf_{\pi,A}\circ S^{\prime}_{A})=\dim\Ker S^{\prime\prime}_{A}
,
for every
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})

and
A:=\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Using this,
conditions (a) and (b) are equivalent to:

(a”)

|I^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta}|\leq|I_{\zeta}|
,
for every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
;
and

(b”)

\Img S^{\prime\prime}_{A}\subseteq\Img S_{A}

and

\dim\Ker S^{\prime\prime}_{A}\leq\dim\Ker S_{A}
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Suppose there is
\theta

such that
(
7.3
)
is semi-cartesian.
As

(\theta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}},\eta^{\prime\prime}_{I^{\prime\prime}},\pi)

is cartesian,
there is a unique homomorphism

\theta^{\prime}\colon H\to H^{\prime\prime}

such that (
7.5
) commutes.
By Lemma
2.8
(b),

\theta^{\prime}
is an epimorphism.
By Theorem
7.1

(a”) and (b”) hold.

Conversely, suppose that
(a”) and (b”) hold.
By Theorem
7.1

there is

\theta^{\prime}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime\prime}

such that
the left-handed square in (
7.5
)
commutes.
Put
\theta=\theta^{\prime\prime}\circ\theta^{\prime}
,
then (
7.3
) commutes.
By Lemma
2.8
(a)
