it is a semi-cartesian square.
∎

Example 7.5
.

With the notation at the beginning of this section
assume that

I^{\prime}\subseteq I

and,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}
,

\eta_{i}=\Inf_{\pi}\eta^{\prime}_{i}
,
so that
there is
a cartesian square

\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.6)

Then

I^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{\zeta}

for every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G^{\prime})
,
so condition (a) of Theorem
7.4
is satisfied.
So is condition (b),
for every
A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G^{\prime})
,
because there is a homomorphism

\theta_{A}^{*}

such that
(
7.4
) commutes.
Indeed,
F_{A}=F_{A^{\prime}}
,
and

V_{A}=\oplus_{i\in I_{A}}F_{A}\varphi_{i}
,
where

S_{A}(\varphi_{i})=\eta_{i}
, for every
i
;
similarly,

V_{A}^{\prime}=\oplus_{i\in I^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}}F_{A}\varphi^{\prime}_{i}
,
where

S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}(\varphi_{i})=\eta^{\prime}_{i}
, for every
i
,
so

\varphi^{\prime}_{i}\mapsto\varphi_{i}
,
for
i\in I^{\prime}
,
defines the required homomorphism

\theta_{A}^{*}
.

Thus, by Theorem
7.4
,
there is

\theta\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}H^{\prime}_{i}

such that
(
7.3
) is a semi-cartesian square.

An explicit definition of
\theta
is:

(h_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto(\rho_{i}(h_{i}))_{i\in I^{\prime}}
.
This map is well defined by the commutativity of (
7.6
)
and extends
\pi
,
that is, the following (not necessarily cartesian) diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.7)

Moreover,
\theta
is an epimorphism.
Indeed,
let
(h^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}\in\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}H^{\prime}_{i}
.
Choose
j\in I^{\prime}
and
h_{j}\in H_{j}
such that

\rho_{j}(h_{j})=h^{\prime}_{j}
.
Put
g=\eta_{j}(h_{j})

and

g^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime}_{j})
.
Then
\pi(g)=g^{\prime}
.
As (
7.6
) is cartesian,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}

there is
h_{i}\in H_{i}

such that

\eta_{i}(h_{i})=g
and
\rho_{i}(h_{i})=h^{\prime}_{i}
.
For every
i\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}
,
there is
h_{i}\in H_{i}

such that

\eta_{i}(h_{i})=g
.
Thus

(h_{i})_{i\in I}\in\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and

(h_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto(h^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
.

Notice that both
\theta
and
\eta_{I}

factor through

\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
so (
7.7
) remains commutative, if

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is replaced by

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
;
then
I^{\prime}=I
.
If
I^{\prime}=I
,
then (
7.7
) is a cartesian square.

If
I^{\prime}=\emptyset
,
then

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H^{\prime}_{i}=G
and
\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
is the identity,
so
\theta=\pi\circ\eta_{I}
,
whence

\Ker\theta=\eta_{I}^{-1}(\Ker\pi)=\{(h_{i})_{i\in I}\in\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}|\,(\forall i\in I)\eta_{i}(h_{i})\in\Ker\pi\}=\\
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{\Ker\pi}}}{{\prod}_{\Ker\pi}}\eta_{i}^{-1}(\Ker\pi).

(7.8)

If
I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
,

\Ker\theta=\big(\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}\Ker\rho_{i}\big)\times_{G}\big(\underset{i\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\big)

and
\eta_{I^{\prime}}\colon\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
maps

\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}\Ker\rho_{i}

isomorphically onto
\Ker\pi
.

Conversely,
every epimorphism from

\theta\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}

onto some profinite group
is essentially of this form.
I.e.,
we show below that
if
L\triangleleft\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
then

(\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i})/L

is a fiber product of indecomposables over

G^{\prime}=G/\eta_{I}(L)
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
(
7.9
)
be a semi-cartesian square.

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.9)

\textstyle{{\bar{H}}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{\rho}}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{\eta}}_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.10)

Then there are families

\bar{\mathcal{H}}=({\bar{\eta}}_{i}\colon{\bar{H}}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

and

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{\eta^{\prime}_{i}\colon H^{\prime}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime})_{i\in I^{\prime}}

of indecomposable epimorphisms
such that

I^{\prime}\subseteq I

and

{\bar{\eta}}_{i}=\Inf_{\pi}(\eta^{\prime}_{i})

with a cartesian square (
7.10
)
for every
i\in I^{\prime}
,
and there are isomorphisms
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}

such that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{\eta}}_{I}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G^{\prime}}}}{{\prod}_{G^{\prime}}}H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.11)

Here

{\bar{\eta}}_{I}

and
\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}

are the structure maps of the corresponding fiber products
and
\theta
is the map

(h_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto({\bar{\rho}}_{i}(h_{i}))_{i\in I^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
L=\Ker\rho

and

K=\Ker\eta_{I}
.
By
Corollary
6.5
,
there is a family

\bar{\mathcal{H}}=({\bar{\eta}}_{i}\colon{\bar{H}}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

of indecomposable epimorphisms
and an isomorphism

\omega\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}

such that

{\bar{\eta}}_{I}\circ\omega=\eta_{I}

and
\omega(K\cap L)=\prod_{i\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}{\bar{K}}_{i}
,
for some
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
.
Here

{\bar{K}}_{j}
is the kernel of the projection

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{j\in I\smallsetminus\{j\}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{j}
,
for every
j\in I
.
So, replacing

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
by
\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}

and
\rho
by
\omega^{-1}\circ\rho

we may assume that

K\cap L=\prod_{j\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}}K_{j}
,
where

K_{j}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{j\}}
.
Thus

K\cap L=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I^{\prime}}
.
Of course, then
(
7.6
) replaces (
7.10
).

We may now replace

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
by
\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

and
\rho
by the induced map

{\underset{i\in I^{\prime}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}}
.
The new square, that replaces (
7.9
),
is still semi-cartesian, by Lemma
2.8
(a).
So we may assume that

K\cap L=1

and

I^{\prime}=I
.
Put
H=\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.

As
K\cap L=1
,
(
7.9
)
is cartesian
and
\rho
is injective on

K=\prod_{i\in I}K_{i}
.
Thus

\rho(K)=\prod_{i\in I}\rho(K_{i})
.
For every
i\in I

let
H^{\prime}_{i}=H^{\prime}/\prod_{j\neq i}\rho(K_{j})
,
let
p_{i}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}_{i}
be the quotient map,
and let
\eta^{\prime}_{i}\colon H^{\prime}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

be the map induced from
\eta^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
1.4
(a),

\Ker\eta^{\prime}_{i}\cong K_{i}
is a minimal normal subgroup of
H^{\prime}_{i}
.
Hence
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
is indecomposable.
By Lemma
3.5
.

H^{\prime}
is the fiber product of
H^{\prime}_{i}
over
G^{\prime}
,
with
\eta^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}_{I}

and
p_{i}
is the coordinate projection,
for every
i\in I
.

Let
\rho_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}_{i}

be the map induced from
\rho\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}
.
Then (
7.12
) below commutes.

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{I}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(7.12)

\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}}
\textstyle{\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{I^{\prime},i}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{i}}
\textstyle{{\bar{H}}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{i}^{-1}}
\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{i}}

(7.13)

Its upper square is cartesian,
because

\rho
maps

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}=\prod_{j\neq i}K_{j}

isomorphically onto

\Ker p_{i}=\prod_{j\neq i}\rho(K_{j})
.
The concatenation of the uppper and the lower square
is the semi-cartesian square (
7.9
),
which is cartesian, because
K\cap L=1
.
Thus the lower square,
that is (
7.6
),
is cartesian by Remark
2.10
(a).
The commutativity of the upper square gives that

\rho
is

(h_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto(\rho_{i}(h_{i}))_{i\in I}
.

If
H_{i}={\bar{H}}_{i}
and
\rho_{i}={\bar{\rho}}_{i}
, for every
i\in I^{\prime}
,
we are done.
If not,
(
7.10
)
and
the lower square in (
7.12
)
are both the fiber products of
\pi
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
.
By the uniqueness of the fiber product,
there is an isomorphism

\omega_{i}\colon H_{i}\to{\bar{H}}_{i}

such that

{\bar{\eta}}_{i}\circ\omega_{i}=\eta_{i}

and

{\bar{\rho}}_{i}\circ\omega_{i}=\rho_{i}
,
for every
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Let
{\bar{H}}=H_{i}
and
\omega_{i}=\mathrm{id}_{H_{i}}

for
i\in I\smallsetminus I^{\prime}

Then

(\omega_{i})_{i\in I}

define an isomorphism

\omega\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\to\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}

such that

{\bar{\eta}}_{I}\circ\omega=\eta_{I}

and
(
7.13
) above
commutes
for every
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Replace

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

by

\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}{\bar{H}}_{i}

and
\rho
by
\rho\circ\omega^{-1}
.
Then
\rho_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}_{i}

in (
7.12
)
is replaced by
{\bar{\rho}}_{i}
,
as required.
∎

8
Fundaments of epimorphisms

We now come to the ‘fundamental’ notion of this work:

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
be an epimorphism of profinite groups.
The
fundament kernel
of
\pi
,
denoted
\mathcal{M}(\pi)
,
is the intersection of all

N\in\mathcal{N}_{\Ker\pi}(H)
,
i.e.,
N
that satisfy the equivalent conditions of
Lemma
1.2

with
M=\Ker\pi
.
Clearly
\mathcal{M}(\pi)\triangleleft H
and
\mathcal{M}(\pi)\leq\Ker\pi
.
An epimorphism

\bar{\pi}\colon{\bar{H}}\twoheadrightarrow G

is the
fundament
of
\pi
,
if it is isomorphic to the quotient map

\bar{\pi}\colon H/\mathcal{M}(\pi)\twoheadrightarrow G
,
that is,
if there is

\rho\colon H\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{H}}

such that

\bar{\pi}\circ\rho=\pi

and

\Ker\rho=\mathcal{M}(\pi)
;
we then also say that

\bar{\pi}
is

the fundament of
\pi
by
\rho
.
We say that
\pi
is
fundamental
,
if
\bar{\pi}=\pi
, that is,
if
\mathcal{M}(\pi)=1
.
Notice that the fundament of an epimorphism
is itself fundamental.

Remark 8.2
.

Here is the Galois-theoretic interpretation of Definition
8.1
:

Let
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
be
Galois extensions of a field
K
,
and let

\pi\colon\Gal(L/K)\twoheadrightarrow\Gal(L^{\prime}/K)

be the restriction map.
Then the fundament of
\pi
is
the restriction

\bar{\pi}\colon\Gal({\bar{L}}/K)\twoheadrightarrow\Gal(L^{\prime}/K)
,
where
{\bar{L}}
is the compositum of all
Galois extensions of
K
contained in
L
, properly containing
L^{\prime}
,
and minimal with respect to these conditions.
The fundament kernel of
\pi
is
\Gal(L/{\bar{L}})
.

The
Melnikov group

of a profinite group
H

is the intersection of
the maximal open normal subgroups of
H

(
[
FJ
, p. 637]
).
Thus
the Melnikov group of
H

is the fundament kernel of
H\twoheadrightarrow 1
.

Using Notation
6.1
we have:

Definition 8.3
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be an epimorphism.

(a)
For every

\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
let

\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}=\{N\triangleleft H|\,N\leq\Ker\pi,\ (H/N\twoheadrightarrow G)\cong_{G}\zeta\}
.
We call
|\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}|

the
\zeta
-multiplicity

of
\pi
,
denoted
\mult_{\zeta}(\pi)
.

Put

N_{\zeta}=\bigcap_{N\in\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}}N

and

H_{\zeta}=H/N_{\zeta}
.

(b)
For every

A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
let

\mathcal{C}_{A}=\{N\triangleleft H|\,N\leq\Ker\pi,\ H/N\cong_{G}A\}
.
Put

N_{A}=\bigcap_{N\in\mathcal{C}_{A}}N

and

H_{A}=H/N_{A}
.

Let
{\bar{\pi}}_{A}\colon H_{A}\twoheadrightarrow G
be the induced map.
Then
B_{A}=\Ker{\bar{\pi}}_{A}

is a
G
-submodule of

\prod_{N\in\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}}H/N
,
and hence,
by Remark
4.1
(b),
is an
A
-generated
G
-module.
Let

f_{A}\in H^{2}(G,B_{A})

be the cocycle corresponding to the extension

1\to B_{A}\to H_{A}\to G\to 1

and let
(B_{A}^{*},S_{A})=X^{2}(B_{A},f_{A})
.
So

B_{A}^{*}
is a vector space over
F_{A}=\End_{G}(A)

and

S_{A}\colon B_{A}^{*}\to H^{2}(G,A)

is the
F_{A}
-linear map

\varphi\mapsto(f_{A})_{*}\varphi
.

We call
\dim\Ker S_{A}
the
A
-multiplicity

of
\pi
,
denoted
\mult_{A}(\pi)
.
We call

\Img S_{A}

the
A
-support

of
\pi
,
denoted
\supp_{A}(\pi)
.

(c)
Furthermore,
let
(v_{i})_{i\in I}

be a family of vectors in a vector space
V
over a field
F
.
Denoting by
F^{I}
the vector space with basis
I
,
let
T\colon F^{I}\to V

be the unique linear map that maps
i
onto
v_{i}
,
for all
i\in I
.
Then

\dim\Ker T
is the
relation dimension

of
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
.

Thus,
if
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}
is a basis of
B_{A}^{*}
in (b),
then
i\mapsto\varphi_{i}
is an isomorphism

F^{I}\cong_{G}B_{A}^{*}
,
and hence

\supp_{A}(\pi)=\Span((\eta_{i})_{i\in I})

and

\mult_{A}(\pi)

is the relation dimension of

(\eta_{i})_{i\in I}
,
where

\eta_{i}=(f_{A})_{*}\varphi_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
.

Example 8.4
.

Let
H
be the fiber product of a family

(\eta_{I}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

of indecomposable epimorphisms
and let
\pi=\eta_{I}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
.
Fix

\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)

and

A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
,
and let

I_{\zeta}=\{i\in I|\,\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\zeta\}

and

I_{A}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}A\}
.

Let
i\in I
. Then

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\in\mathcal{N}_{\Ker\pi}(H)

and

H/\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\twoheadrightarrow H/\Ker\pi

is

\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
.
Therefore,

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}

if and only if

i\in I_{\zeta}
.
Similarly,

\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}\in\mathcal{C}_{A}

if and only if

i\in I_{A}
.

As

\mathcal{M}(\pi)\leq\bigcap_{j\in I}\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,j}=1
,
\pi
is fundamental.

By Theorem
6.7
(a’),
every
N\in\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}
is
\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}
for some
i\in I
,
hence

\mult_{\zeta}(\pi)=|\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}|=|I_{\zeta}|
.

We have

\bigcap_{i\in I_{A}}\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,i}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}
.
By Theorem
6.7
(b’),
every
N\in\mathcal{C}_{A}
contains
\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}
,
hence

N_{A}=\bigcap_{N\in\mathcal{C}_{A}}N=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I_{A}}
,
whence

H/N_{A}=\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
.
Therefore

B_{A}=\Ker(\eta_{I_{A}}\colon\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)
.
For every
i\in I_{A}
we have

\eta_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{I_{A},i}=\eta_{I_{A}}
,
hence by diagram (
5.5
) in
Construction
5.7

we have

S_{A}(\mathrm{pr}_{I_{A},i})=\eta_{i}
.
But

(\mathrm{pr}_{I_{A},i})_{i\in I_{A}}

is a basis of
B_{A}^{*}
,
hence

\supp_{A}(\pi)=\Span(\eta_{i}|\,i\in I_{A})
,
and

\mult_{A}(\pi)=\Ker S_{A}

is the relation dimension of

(\eta_{i})_{i\in I_{A}}
.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
be
a fundamental epimorphism.
Then

H\cong_{G}\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\lambda}\ \big[=(\underset{\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{\zeta})\times_{G}(\underset{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{A})\big]
.
Moreover:

(a)

For every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
,

H_{\zeta}\cong_{G}\underset{i\in I_{\zeta}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
where

|I_{\zeta}|=|\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}|

and

{\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G}

is isomorphic to

\zeta
,
for every
i\in I_{\zeta}
.

(b)

For every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

let

\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in I_{A}}
be a basis of
B_{A}^{*}

over
F_{A}=\End_{G}(A)
.
Then

H_{A}\cong_{G}\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
where

\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G

is

(\varphi_{i})_{*}f_{A}=S_{A}(\varphi_{i})\in H^{2}(G,A)
,
for every
i\in I_{A}
.

Thus

H
is the fiber product
of the family

(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

of indecomposable extensions of
G
,
where

I=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)}I_{\zeta}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}I_{A}
.

Proof.

Let
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
.
Write

\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}=\{N_{i}\}_{i\in I_{\zeta}}
.
For every
i\in I_{\zeta}

put
H_{i}=H/N_{i}

and let
\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G
be the map induced from

\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
.
Then

\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\zeta

and

H_{\zeta}=H/(\bigcap_{i\in I_{\zeta}}N_{i})
.
Therefore

H_{\zeta}\to\underset{i\in I_{\zeta}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is an isomorphism by
Proposition
6.10
.

Let
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Then
B_{A}
is an
A
-generated
G
-module.
For every
i\in I_{A}

let
\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G

be the extension represented by

(\varphi_{i})_{*}f_{A}=S_{A}(\varphi_{i})\in H^{2}(G,A)
.
Then

H_{A}\to\underset{i\in I_{A}\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

is an isomorphism
by Corollary
5.10
.

By
Remark
3.2
(d),

\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}\kern-3.0ptH_{\lambda}\cong_{G}\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}
,
where

I=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)}I_{\zeta}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)}I_{A}
.
Thus,

H\to\kern-3.0pt\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda(G)\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}\kern-5.0ptH_{\lambda}

is an isomorphism
by Proposition
6.10
.
∎

From Proposition
8.5

and Example
8.4

we get:

Corollary 8.6
.

An epimorphism
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

is fundamental
if and only if it is isomorphic to
the structure map
of the fiber product
of indecomposable extensions of
G
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
be fundamental
and
let

\eta_{I}\colon\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G

be the fiber product
of a family
of indecomposable extensions

(\eta_{i}\colon H_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I}

of
G
.
Denote

I_{\zeta}=\{i\in I|\,\eta_{i}\cong_{G}\zeta\}
,
for every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
,
and

I_{A}=\{i\in I|\,\Ker\eta_{i}\cong_{G}A\}
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Then
\pi\cong_{G}\eta_{I}

if and only if

(a)

|I_{\zeta}|=\mult_{\zeta}(\pi)
,
for every
\zeta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
,

(b)

\Span(\eta_{i})_{i\in I_{A}}=\supp_{A}(\pi)
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
,
and

(c)

\mult_{A}(\pi)

is the relation dimension of

(\eta_{i})_{i\in I_{A}}
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Proof.

If
\pi\cong_{G}\eta_{I}
,
then
(a), (b), (c) hold
by Example
8.4
.

Conversely,
assume that
(a), (b), (c) hold.
By
Corollary
8.6

we may assume that

H
is the fiber product of a family

(\eta^{\prime}_{i}\colon H^{\prime}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow G)_{i\in I^{\prime}}

of indecomposable extensions of
G
.
If we define

I^{\prime}_{\zeta}
and
I^{\prime}_{A}

for
\pi
, analogously to

I_{\zeta}
and
I_{A}
for
\eta_{I}
,
then, again
by Example
8.4
,
conditions (a), (b) of
Corollary
7.2
are satisfied.
Thus

\pi\cong_{G}\eta_{I}
.
∎

For an indecomposable cover
\eta
of
G

and a cardinality
\kappa

let

\eta^{(\kappa)}

denote the fiber product of
\kappa
copies of
\eta
.

Example 8.8
.

Let
\eta
be an indecomposable cover of
G

and
\kappa
a cardinality.

(a)
If
\kappa=0
,
then
\eta^{(\kappa)}
is the identity of
G
,
hence

\mult_{\lambda}(\eta^{(\kappa)})=0

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)

and

\supp_{A}(\eta^{(\kappa)})=0

for all
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

Assume that

\kappa>0
.
Put
C=\Ker\eta
.

(b)
If
C
is non-abelian,
then
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)

and all
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

\mult_{\lambda}(\eta^{(\kappa)})=\begin{cases}\kappa&\textnormal{if $\lambda=\eta$}\\
0&\textnormal{if $\lambda\neq\eta$}\end{cases},\qquad\supp_{A}(\eta^{(\kappa)})=0.

(This is true even if
\kappa=0
, by (a).)

(c)
If
C
is abelian,
then

\mult_{\lambda}(\eta^{(\kappa)})=0

for every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
.
By Example
5.9
,

\dim B_{C}^{*}=\kappa

and

\Img S_{C}=\Span(\eta)
,
while

\dim B_{A}^{*}=0

for all
C\neq A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
We have

\dim B_{A}^{*}=\dim\Img S_{A}+\dim\Ker S_{A}
,
for all
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Thus for all
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)

and all
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

\displaystyle\mult_{\lambda}(\eta^{(\kappa)})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\textnormal{if $\lambda\neq C$}\\
\kappa&\textnormal{if $\lambda=C$ and $\eta$ splits}\\
\kappa-1&\textnormal{if $\lambda=C$, $\eta$ does not split,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\supp_{A}(\eta^{(\kappa)})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\textnormal{if $A\neq C$}\\
\Span(\eta)&\textnormal{if $A=C$.}\end{cases}

Corollary 8.9
.

Let
\tau\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

and

\tau^{\prime}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

be fundamental.

Let
\pi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

be an epimorphism.
There is
\theta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}

such that

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(8.1)

is semi-cartesian
if and only if

(a)

\mult_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})\leq\mult_{\zeta}(\tau)
,
for every

\zeta^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G^{\prime})

and

\zeta:=\Inf_{\pi}(\zeta^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
;

(b)

\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}(\supp_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}))\subseteq\supp_{A}(\tau)

for every

A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
and

A:=\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
;
and

(c)

\nu_{\pi,A^{\prime}}+\mult_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})\leq\mult_{A}(\tau)
,
for every

A^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
and

A:=\Inf_{\pi}(A^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
;
here

\nu_{\pi,A^{\prime}}
is the nullity of the restriction

r\colon\supp_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})\to\supp_{A}(\tau)

of

\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}
to
\supp_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This is reinterpretation of Theorem
7.4
:
By Proposition
8.5

we may assume that

\tau=\eta_{I}

and

\tau^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}_{I^{\prime}}
,
in the notation of Theorem
7.4

(fixed at the beginning of Section
7
),
and then

\Img S_{A}=\supp_{A}(\tau)
,
and

\Img(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}(\Img S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}(\supp_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})).

Moreover,
if

\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}(\supp_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}))\subseteq\supp_{A}(\tau)
,
then

\Ker(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=\Ker(r\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=(S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})^{-1}(\Ker r),

hence

\dim\Ker(\Inf_{\pi,A^{\prime}}\circ S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}})=\dim\Ker S^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}+\dim\Ker r=\mult_{A^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})+\nu_{\pi,A^{\prime}}.

∎

Corollary 8.10
.

Let
\tau\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

and

\tau^{\prime}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G

be fundamental.

(1)

\tau^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\tau

if and only if

(1a)

\mult_{\lambda}(\tau^{\prime})\leq\mult_{\lambda}(\tau)
,
for every
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)
;
and

(1b)

\supp_{A}(\tau^{\prime})\subseteq\supp_{A}(\tau)
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.

(2)

\tau^{\prime}\cong_{G}\tau

if and only if

(2a)

\mult_{\lambda}(\tau^{\prime})=\mult_{\lambda}(\tau)
,
for every
\lambda\in\Lambda(G)
;
and

(2b)

\supp_{A}(\tau^{\prime})=\supp_{A}(\tau)
,
for every
A\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)

Proof.

(1) is reinterpretation of Theorem
7.1
.

(2) is reinterpretation of Corollary
7.2
.
∎

Applying the criterion of Corollary
8.10
(1)
to Example
8.8
we get

Corollary 8.11
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
be fundamental.
Let
\eta
be an indecomposable cover of
G

and
\kappa\geq 0
a cardinality.
Put
C=\Ker\eta
.

(a)

If
C
is not abelian, then

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi

if and only if

\kappa\leq\mult_{\eta}(\pi)
.

(b)

If
C\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
, then

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi

if and only if

\begin{cases}\kappa\leq\mult_{C}(\pi)&\textnormal{if $\eta\in\supp_{C}(\pi)$ splits}\\
\kappa\leq\mult_{C}(\pi)+1&\textnormal{if $\eta\in\supp_{C}(\pi)$ does not split}\\
\kappa=0&\textnormal{if $\eta\notin\supp_{C}(\pi)$}\end{cases}

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

and

\pi^{\prime}\colon H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

be fundamental.
Then

\pi^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\pi

if and only if

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi
,
for
every indecomposable cover
\eta
of
G

and
every
\kappa
.

Proof.

If

\pi^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\pi
,
then the condition holds
by the transitivity of
\preccurlyeq
.

Conversely, assume that
the condition holds.

Claim A:

Let
C\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Then

\supp_{C}(\pi^{\prime})\subseteq\supp_{C}(\pi)
.

Indeed,
let
\eta\in H^{2}(G,C)\smallsetminus\supp_{C}(\pi)
;
in particular,
\eta\neq 0
, so
\eta
does not split.
By Corollary
8.11
(b),

\eta=\eta^{(1)}\not\preccurlyeq\pi
,
hence

\eta^{(1)}\not\preccurlyeq\pi^{\prime}
.
As

1\leq\mult_{C}(\pi^{\prime})+1
,
this implies,
again by Corollary
8.11
(b),
that

\eta\notin\supp_{C}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Claim B:

Let
\eta\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{na}}(G)
.
Then

\mult_{\eta}(\pi^{\prime})\leq\mult_{\eta}(\pi)
.

Put

\kappa=\mult_{\eta}(\pi^{\prime})
.
By Corollary
8.11
(a),

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi^{\prime}
,
hence

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi
,
whence, again
by Corollary
8.11
(a),

\kappa\leq\mult_{\eta}(\pi)
.

Claim C:

Let
C\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ab}}(G)
.
Then

\mult_{C}(\pi^{\prime})\leq\mult_{C}(\pi)
.

Put

\kappa=\mult_{C}(\pi^{\prime})
and
let
\eta
be the split extension of
G
with kernel
C
.
By Corollary
8.11
(b),

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi^{\prime}
,
hence

\eta^{(\kappa)}\preccurlyeq\pi
,
whence, again
by Corollary
8.11
(b),

\kappa\leq\mult_{C}(\pi)
.

Thus

\pi^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\pi

by Corollary
8.10
(1).
∎

Corollary 8.13
.

Let

\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be a fundamental epimorphism
and let
L
be a normal subgroup of
H
,
contained in
\Ker\pi
.
Then there is an isomorphism

\theta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I\phantom{\,{G}}}{{\prod}_{G}}H_{i}

onto a fiber product of
indecomposable covers of
G

such that

\theta(L)=\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}K_{i}
,
for some

I^{\prime}\subseteq I
,
where

K_{i}=\Ker\mathrm{pr}_{I,I\smallsetminus\{i\}}
,
for every
i\in I
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.5

we may assume that

\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

is the fiber product of indecomposable covers of
G
.
So the assertion follows by Corollary
6.5
.
∎

Corollary 8.14
.

In a semi-cartesian square (
8.1
),
if
\pi
is fundamental, then so is
\pi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.5
,

H
is the fiber product of indecomposable extensions of
G
.
As (
8.1
) is semi-cartesian,

\Ker\theta_{0}=\pi(\Ker\theta)
.
Hence, by Proposition
7.6
,

H^{\prime}
is the fiber product of indecomposable extensions of
G^{\prime}
.
By Corollary
8.6
,
\pi^{\prime}
is fundamental.
∎

Proposition 8.15
.

Let

\rho\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G_{1}
,

{\bar{\pi}}\colon G_{1}\twoheadrightarrow G
,
and
\pi={\bar{\pi}}\circ\rho\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

be epimorphisms.
Assume that

{\bar{\pi}}
is fundamental.
Then

{\bar{\pi}}
is the fundament of
\pi
by
\rho

if and only if
there is no
commutative diagram

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\bar{\pi}}}
\textstyle{H_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{0}}
\textstyle{G}

(8.2)

with
a semi-cartesian square
and
indecomposable
\eta_{0}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
there is a semi-cartesian square
(
8.2
)
with
\eta_{0}
indecomposable.
Then

(\Ker\gamma)(\Ker\rho)=\Ker\pi
.
As
\pi=\eta_{0}\circ\gamma

and
\eta_{0}
is not an isomorphism,

\Ker\pi
strictly contains
\Ker\gamma
.
Hence

\Ker\rho\not\leq\Ker\gamma
.
But
\Ker\gamma\in\mathcal{N}_{\Ker\pi}(H)
,
hence

\Ker\rho\neq\mathcal{M}(\pi)
.

Conversely,
suppose that

\Ker\rho\neq\mathcal{M}(\pi)
.

As
{\bar{\pi}}\colon G_{1}\twoheadrightarrow G
is fundamental,
the intersection of the groups in

\{{\bar{N}}\triangleleft G_{1}|\,{\bar{N}}\leq\Ker{\bar{\pi}},\ G_{1}/{\bar{N}}\twoheadrightarrow G\textnormal{ is indecomposable}\}

is
1
.
Taking the inverse image of this set under
\rho
we get that
the intersection of the groups in

\mathcal{F}=\{N\triangleleft H|\,\Ker\rho\leq N\leq\Ker\pi,\ H/N\twoheadrightarrow G\textnormal{ is indecomposable}\}

is
\Ker\rho
.
But
\mathcal{M}(\pi)
is the intersection of the groups in

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\{N\triangleleft H|\,N\leq\Ker\pi,\ H/N\twoheadrightarrow G\textnormal{ is indecomposable}\}.

So

\mathcal{F}\neq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Clearly

\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Thus there is

N\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

such that

\Ker\rho\not\leq N
,
that is,

N
is properly contained in

N\Ker\rho
.
As
N
is a maximal normal subgroup of
H

contained in
\Ker\pi
,
we have

N\Ker\rho=\Ker\pi
.

Put

H_{0}=H/N
,
let
\gamma\colon H\twoheadrightarrow H_{0}
be the quotient map,
and let

\eta_{0}\colon H_{0}\twoheadrightarrow G

be the epimorphism induced from
\pi
.
Then (
8.2
) is a semi-cartesian square.
∎

9
Fundament series

Definition 9.1
.

The
fundament kernel series
of
an epimorphism
\pi\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G

of profinite groups
is the descending sequence
of normal subgroups of
H

M_{0}(\pi)\geq M_{1}(\pi)\geq M_{2}(\pi)\geq\cdots

inductively defined by

M_{0}(\pi)=\Ker\pi
,
and
M_{i}(\pi)

is the fundament kernel of the quotient map

H\twoheadrightarrow H/M_{i-1}(\pi)
,
for each
i\geq 1
,
that is,

M_{i}(\pi)
is the intersection of all

N\in\mathcal{N}_{M_{i-1}(\pi)}(H)
.
In particular,

M_{1}(H)=\mathcal{M}(\pi)
.

Put
G_{i}=H/M_{i}(\pi)
for each
i\geq 0
.
The sequence of the induced quotient maps

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{3}}
\textstyle{G_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{G_{0}\hbox to0.0pt{ $=G$\hss}}

is the
fundament series
of
\pi
.
We call
\pi_{k}

the
k
-th fundament
of
\pi
.
Thus

\pi_{k}
is the fundament of the quotient map

H\twoheadrightarrow G_{k-1}
.

Proposition 9.2
.

We have
\bigcap_{i=0}^{\infty}M_{i}(\pi)=1

and hence
H=\varprojlim_{i}G_{i}
.

Proof.

Put
M_{i}=M_{i}(\pi)
for each
i
.

The second assertion follows from the first one.
To prove the first one,
it suffices to show, for every open
U\triangleleft H
,
that there is
i
such that
M_{i}\leq U
,
that is,
M_{i}U=U
.
Thus, as
H/U
is finite,
it suffices to show that
if
M_{i-1}U\neq U
,
then

M_{i}U<M_{i-1}U
.

So put
M=M_{i-1}
and assume
MU\neq U
.
Let
\rho\colon H\twoheadrightarrow{\bar{H}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=H/U
be the quotient map.
By assumption,

{\bar{M}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\rho(M)\neq 1
,
hence, by Remark
1.3
(d),
there is
{\bar{N}}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bar{M}}({\bar{H}})
.
By Lemma
1.4
(c)
there is
N\in\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)

such that
\rho(N)={\bar{N}}
.

By the inductive definition of
M_{i}

we have
M_{i}\leq N
.
It follows that

M_{i}U/U=\rho(M_{i})\leq{\bar{N}}<{\bar{M}}=M_{i-1}U/U
,
that is,

M_{i}U<M_{i-1}U
.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{0}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}}

(9.1)

be a commutative diagram.
Then

\theta(M_{k}(\pi))\leq M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
,
for every
k\geq 0
.
If (
9.1
) is semi-cartesian,
then

\theta(M_{k}(\pi))=M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
,
for every
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

By induction on
k
.
For
k=0

the assertions follow from definitions.
Let
k\geq 1
.
Put

M=M_{k-1}(\pi)

and

M^{\prime}=M_{k-1}(\pi^{\prime})
,
and
assume that

\theta(M)\leq M^{\prime}
.
Then

M\leq\theta^{-1}(M^{\prime})
.

By definition,

M_{k}(\pi)=\bigcap_{N\in\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)}N

and

M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})=\bigcap_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{M^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})}N^{\prime}
.

For

N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{M^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})

let

N=\theta^{-1}(N^{\prime})
.
Then

N\in\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{-1}(M^{\prime})}(H)
.
If
M\leq N
, then

M_{k}(\pi)\leq M\leq N
.
If

M\not\leq N
,
then,
as
N
is a maximal normal subgroup of
H

contained in
\theta^{-1}(M^{\prime})
,
we have

NM=\theta^{-1}(M^{\prime})
.
Thus

N\in\mathcal{N}_{NM}(H)
,
whence

M\cap N\in\mathcal{N}_{M}(H)
.
Therefore

M_{k}(\pi)\leq M\cap N\leq N
.

Thus,

M_{k}(\pi)\leq\bigcap_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{M^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})}\theta^{-1}(N^{\prime})=\theta^{-1}(M_{k}(\pi^{\prime}))
.
It follows that

\theta(M_{k}(\pi))\leq M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Now assume that

\theta(M)=M^{\prime}
.
Let
L^{\prime}=\theta(M_{k}(\pi))
.
Then, by the above,

L^{\prime}\leq M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})\leq M^{\prime}

and
L^{\prime}\triangleleft H^{\prime}
,
and the square

\textstyle{H/M_{k}(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}/L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H/M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}/M^{\prime}}

with
vertical quotient maps
and horizontal maps induced from
\theta
,
is semi-cartesian.
Hence, by Corollary
8.14
,

H^{\prime}/L^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}/M^{\prime}

is fundamental.
Therefore

L^{\prime}
is the intersection of groups in

\mathcal{N}_{M^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})
,
and hence

L^{\prime}\geq M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Thus
L^{\prime}=M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
.
∎

When, instead of normal subgroups,
we consider the quotient maps modulo these subgroups,
we get the following equivalent formulation:

Theorem 9.4
.

Let (
9.1
)
be a commutative diagram.
Let

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{3}}

\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{G_{0}=G}

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}

of

and

, respectively.
Then

(a)

there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k+1}}
\textstyle{G_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{k}}
\textstyle{G_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{3}}

\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}_{k-1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{k+1}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{k}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{k-1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{3}}

\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{0}}

(9.2)

(b)

If
(
9.1
)
is a semi-cartesian square,
then so are the squares

(\rho_{k},\theta_{k},\theta,\rho^{\prime}_{k})

and

(\pi_{k+1},\theta_{k},\theta_{k+1},\pi^{\prime}_{k+1})

for every
k\geq 0
.

(c)

If
\theta_{0}
and
\theta

are isomorphisms,
then so is
\theta_{k}
,
for every
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

(a)
For every
k\geq 0
we have

G_{k}=H/M_{k}(\pi)

and

G^{\prime}_{k}=H^{\prime}/M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
,
and
\pi_{k}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{k}
,
\rho_{k}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{k}

are the quotient maps.
As
\theta(M_{k}(\pi))\leq M_{k}(\pi^{\prime})
,

\theta
induces the
\theta_{k}
such that the diagram commutes.

(b)
If

(\pi,\theta_{0},\theta,\pi^{\prime})

is semi-cartesian,
then so are

(\rho_{k},\theta_{k},\theta,\rho^{\prime}_{k})
,
by the second assertion of Lemmma
9.3
.
By Lemmma
2.11
,
so are

(\pi_{k+1},\theta_{k},\theta_{k+1},\pi^{\prime}_{k+1})
.

(c)
By (b),

(\rho_{k},\theta_{k},\theta,\rho^{\prime}_{k})

is semi-cartesian.
As

\theta
is an isomorphism,
so is
\theta_{k}
.
∎

For the next proposition we use the following setup.
Let

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{3}}
\textstyle{G_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{G_{0}\hbox to0.0pt{ $=G$\hss}}

(9.3)

be a sequence of fundamental epimorphisms.
Let
H=\varprojlim_{i}G_{i}
,
and let
\rho_{i}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}
be the maps
of the inverse limit,
so that

\rho_{i-1}=\pi_{i}\circ\rho_{i}
,
for every
i\geq 1
.
Also, for all
\ell>k
, let

\pi_{\ell,k}=\pi_{k+1}\circ\cdots\circ\pi_{\ell}\colon G_{\ell}\twoheadrightarrow G_{k}
.
Put
\pi=\rho_{0}\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G
.
We then ask,
when is (
9.3
)
the fundament series of
\pi
?

Proposition 9.5
.

In the above setup
the following are equivalent:

(a)

for no
k\geq 1
there is a semi-cartesian square
(
9.4
)
with indecomposable
\eta_{k-1}
.

(b)

for no
\ell\geq k
there is a semi-cartesian square
(
9.5
)
with indecomposable
\eta_{k-1}
.

(c)

for no
k\geq 1
there is a semi-cartesian square
(
9.6
)
with indecomposable
\eta_{k-1}
.

(d)

(
9.3
)
is the fundament series of
\pi
.

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\textstyle{H_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{k-1}}

(9.4)

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{\ell}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\ell,k-1}}
\textstyle{H_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{k-1}}

(9.5)

\textstyle{G_{k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{G_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\textstyle{H_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{k-1}}

(9.6)

Proof.

It will be simpler to work with the negations
(
\neg
a), (
\neg
b), (
\neg
c), (
\neg
d)
of
(a), (b), (c), (d),
respectively.

(
\neg
a)
\Rightarrow
(
\neg
b):
Put
\ell=k
.

(
\neg
b)
\Rightarrow
(
\neg
a):
As
\pi_{\ell,k-1}=\pi_{k}\circ\pi_{\ell,k}
,
this follows by
Lemma
2.11
(a).

(
\neg
a)
\Rightarrow
(
\neg
c):
Suppose
there is a semi-cartesian square
(
9.4
)
with indecomposable
\eta_{k-1}
.

As
\Ker\eta_{k-1}
is finite,
that is,

1
is open in
\Ker\eta_{k-1}
,
we get that

\Ker\sigma=\sigma^{-1}(1)

is open in

\Ker\rho_{k-1}=\sigma^{-1}(\Ker\eta_{k-1})
.
But

\bigcap_{m}\Ker\rho_{m}=1
,
hence
there is
m>k
such that

\Ker\rho_{m}\leq\Ker\sigma
.
Thus there is an epimorphism

\delta_{k-1}\colon G_{m}\twoheadrightarrow H_{k-1}

such that

\sigma=\delta_{k-1}\circ\rho_{m}
,
whence

\eta_{k-1}\circ\delta_{k-1}=\pi_{m,k-1}
.

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{m,k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{H_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{k-1}}

Form the following diagram
(without the dotted arrows)

\textstyle{G_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{m}}
\textstyle{H_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{m}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{i+2}}
\textstyle{H_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{H_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\beta_{k}}

\textstyle{H_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{k-1}}
\textstyle{G_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{m}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i+2}}
\textstyle{G_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i+1}}
\textstyle{G_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{G_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i-1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k+1}}
\textstyle{G_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\textstyle{G_{k-1}}

in which,
recursively,

H_{i}
is the fiber product

G_{i}\times_{G_{i-1}}H_{i-1}

with respect to

\pi_{i}
and
\eta_{i-1}
,
for every
k\leq i\leq m
.
The universal property of a fiber product
gives a homomorphism
\delta_{i}
,
recursively for every
k-1\leq i\leq m
,
such that the above diagram commutes.

The cartesian squares in the diagram
with sides

\pi_{k},\ldots,\pi_{m}

cannot be all compact.
Indeed, if all of them were compact,
then, recursively,

\delta_{k-1},\ldots,\delta_{m}

would be epimorphisms.
In particular,

\delta_{m}
would be an epimorphism,
whence
\eta_{m}

would be an isomorphism,
a contradiction, since, recursively,
the
\eta_{i}
are indecomposable
and hence not isomorphisms.

Let
k\leq i<m

such that
the square with side
\pi_{i+1}
is not compact.
By Proposition
2.12

there is

\gamma_{i}\colon G_{i+1}\twoheadrightarrow H_{i}

such that

\pi_{i+1}=\eta_{i}\circ\gamma_{i}
.
Put

\tau=\beta_{i}\circ\gamma_{i}\colon G_{i+1}\twoheadrightarrow H_{i-1}
.
This gives diagram (
9.6
),
with
i
instead of

.
As
\gamma_{i}
is surjective,
by Lemma
2.8
(a)
this square is semi-cartesian.

(
\neg
c)
\Rightarrow
(
\neg
a):
Diagram
(
9.6
) gives diagram
(
9.4
),
with
\sigma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\tau\circ\rho_{k+1}
.
It is semi-cartesian by Lemma
2.8
(a).

(d)
\Leftrightarrow
(a):
By definition,
(d) means that

\pi_{k}
is the fundament of
\rho_{k-1}
by
\rho_{k}
,
for every
k\geq 1
.
By Lemma
8.15

this is equivalent to (a).
∎
